COUNTY OF CHESTER. THE MARGARINE ACT 1887. AND THE SALE OF FOOD & DRUGS ACT, 1899. NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN that the Local Govern- ment Board have by General Order No. 40834, and dated the 28th February, 1900, directed the manner in which the Registration of Manufactories of Margarine or Margarine Cheese and the premises wherein the business of a wholesale dealer in Margarine or Margarine Cheese is carried on should be given effect to and which Order provides as follows : ARTICLE I.—This Order shall come into operation forthwith, and shall remain in force until We shall otherwit e direct ; and the Order of 1887 is hereby re- scinded : Provided that in every case where a Manufactory of Margarine, or an owner or occupier carrying on the manufacture of Margarine has been duly registered,and such registration has affect at the date of this Order, such registration shall, notwithstanding the rescission of the Order of 1887 but subject to the provisions of Article IV. of this Order, continuo to have effect : Pro- vided also, that any book which prior te the date of this Order has been used by a local authority in pursu- ance of the Order of 1887 for purposes of registration shall, HO far as such hook contains any entry applicable to any such caee as aforesaid, be deemed to be part of the register of the local authority for the purposes of this Order. ARTICLE 11.—Every oemer or cacupier of a Manu- factory of and every whelesale dealer in Margarine or Margarine-cheese in England and Wales who shall make application to the proper local authority for a Certifi- cate of Registration under the Act of 187 and the Act of 1899, or under either of the said Acts, shall, in his application, state the following particulars : (a.) The name and addreas of the owner or occupier or wholesale dealer reeking the application. (ir.) The situatlon of the manufactory, or of the premises wherein the business of the wholesale dealer is carried on. (e.) The name and address, or name a and addresses, of the owner or ownees, or occupier or occupiers, or wholesale dealer sir dealers, carrying on the manu- facture or the business. Every such application shall be signed by the person making the same, or by someone acting on his behalf. ARTICLE the a.ppiication is in due form, the local authority shall cause the Manufactory, or the Premises, as the case may be, to be registered by enter- ing in a book the particulars of the application for regis- tration; and thereupon a Certificate in the Form A. set forth in the Schedule hereto, shall be issued by the local authority to the person applying for the same. ARTICLE IV.—Wh re any change occurs in the persons carrying on the manufacture, or the business, written notice thereof shall be given by the owner or occupier of the Manufactory, or by the wholesale dealer carrying on the business, to the local authority, and the Register shall thereupon be amended by making therein the requisite alteration, and an endorsement shall be made by the local:Authority on the Certificate in :accordance with the Form B, set for; h in the said Schedule. The Local Authority for the Administrative County of Chester, excluding the municipal Borough., of Congloton Hyde, Macclesfield, and Stalvbridge, is the Cheshire County Couucil, and all arpticatione for the Registra- tion of Manufactories of Margarine or Margarine Cheese, or any premises wherein the business of a wholesale dealer in Margarine or Margarine Cheese is carried on must be made to the undersigned, the Clerk to such Local Authority, when it such application is in order a Certificate of such Registration in the form prescribed by the foregoieg Order will be forwarded to the person applying for the satllf.. REGINALD POTTS, Clerk to the Chelhire County Council. Chester, 20th March 1900.

LADIES' LETTTAR._

MY DEAR NELLIE, It appears that many people are thinking ot having costumes made for the c‘ituing spring, with the result that the blouse con.sidered as a separate article is gettin,; in the background. Still, the blouse is far from being eliminated, and there remains many a handsome one. In one that I saw recently the bodice was pointed at the back and front, and round the upper part of the low neck was a very wide guimpe or tucker of guipure, unlined and transparent, showing the neck, the sleeves being the same. In another, the bodice was a bolero, short at the back and long in front, with revers and collar of cerise panne, embroidered with jet beads. There was a small front showing pleated taffetas.

Recent excursions have convinced me that some of the very prettiest of the spring hats are woven in two shades of coloured or fawn straw and trimmed with tall loops of glace silk, matching the deeper tone in the straw. There is usually a little folded band of vivid colour tucked away under the brim, in relief to the fawn and cream. Foliage toques are in favour at the moment. Ivy leaves in every shade of green, the black berries of the plant makin.g, a telling contrast, cover the crowns, and the relief of velvet and feathers is also afforded. The toque is still perhaps the most fashionable headgear, but toques now have become so large and wide that it is difficult to define the line where the toque stops and the hat begins. The latter, where it is worn, is usually of the picture order. All trimmings are low and closely set together; anything approaching height in a hat garniture is old-fashioned. Flowers and ribbon b4;tws are the chief trimmings.

Just at the moment panne is also very muels used for facing the revers of smart cloth coats. A bright shade of turquoise blue forms the ground of one I saw yesterday which was flowered with blossoms and leaves in faint colourings. Another was bright spring green, with red-brown leaves and small yellow rosebuds forming the design. There is an absolute rage for panne. The separate bodice or blouse is now made with quite a variety of materials, and amongst them may be mentioned as suitable surah, glace, or bengaline silk. The cordings which cross the shoulders run right down the back, but are closely set at the waist, and narrow the figure. The lace plastron is laid on a white foundation, and the velvet simulates a vest fastening over the bust.

The bolero is almost becoming monotonous front the unfailing regularity with which it shows itself on all, or nearly all, new dresses. For lighter spring wear it appears in lace and insertion, and a pretty variety is formed of a lattice work of narrow velvet or satin ribbon. This is especially pretty over a bright-coloured underbodice, the interstices of the ribbon showing the colour through. Pastel shades are not to have it all their own way after all. Certain bright tones are gaining ground. A real sky-blue (not the torquoise tint), old rose, and cerise, emerald green, and violet are to find favour during the summer months. _ Flowers are greatly used for the decoration of evening frocks, especially in the form of wreaths and garlands. Small pompon roses strung together are hung in festoons all over the skirts of tulle dresses, and the fashion is a peculiarly charming one for young girls. Velvet and cloth go admirably together, the velvet as a tunic or petticoat, with a repetition in the form of a sleeved bolero. The bolero, as rule, is apt to be a thought too rigid in its lines, but in one that is not there are turned back little fronts, softened by choux of chiffon just where such rosettes look their prettiest. The short velvet straps that bind the fulness of the vest in place are also very new, and the pre- cise curve of the tunic has only to be realised to be admired.

Your loving . JtYirri.

"WOMEN'S CHAT."

The new Rotel yacht, which met with an alarming accident soon after her launch, does not appear to come up to expectation. The vessel, fitted, with all modern improvements, was designed to take the place of theold I Victoria and Altert, but it is doubtful it she will ever I be used for the purpose originally intended. In naval circled it is believed that she will be converted into an Admiralty yacht, to take the place of the present "En- chantress," which is alscesomewhat out of date, and too small for the requirements of the " rulers of the Queen's navee." In her forthcoming visit to Ireland, the Queen will cross St. George's Channel on board the " Victoria and Altert "and this yacht will probably continue to be ussd for any sea journeys undertaken by Her Majesty. The sympathy and beet wishes of the nation will go cut to Lady Roberts, who sailed the other day on board the Dunottar Castle for South Africa. Before leaving, the Quoen personally presented to Lady Roberts the Victoria. Cress, bestowed upon her son. the late Hon. Frederick Rotate, and though naturally rejoicine, over her husband's brilliant achievements, Lady Roberts is really bent upon the same mournful journey as took the ex-Empress Eugenie to almost the same spot in South Africa many years ago. The special object of Lads, Roberts' visit to South Africa is to se toe grave of her son,and the place where he so gallantly met his death. She is accempanied by her two daughters, the Ron. Aileen Roberts, and the Hon. Ada Roberts, and in view of the probable early termination of the war, the Commander-in-Chief will almost certainly jein his family before their return home. Lord and Laely Roberts have passed a happy ' married life, of over forty years. She is herself the daughter of a soldier. and Lord Roberts, though he was born at Cawnpore in 1832, is a member of a family that I'lB for gensratiens hen connected with the court y of Waterford in Ireland. The sale ot the Irish Industries Association which was opened the other day at he Mension House, London, by the Prince and Princess of Wales. has proved one of the most successful held by the Society. The Asiociation is not a charity, but an organisation which has been at work for over ten years, for promoting the sale of home made goods, such as laces, hosiery, embroideries, linen, honsespuna, friezes, and various other articles. It is very s miler to the Scottish Home Industries Association, which ie holdinr an exhibition and sale at Liverpool this month. For several years past, the Queen has invariably made considerable eurchases at these sales, in which many well mown members of society take an interest. There ought indeed to be no d.fficulty in securing a larger amount ef patronaue every year, seeing that the articles on sale really offer first class value for the money, and there is alav tbe inducement that the encouragement of these small industries is of great benefit to the indus- trious peasant. Machinery and education have of course done much to cripple many of the old cottage industries which were formerly carried on to a much larger extent in the country districts than is now the case. Many have been driven out by the cheap productions of the factory.with which the single pair of hands of the cottagers cannot hope to compete upon anything like a commercial basis. Nor is it altogether desirable' that they should attempt such conditions, as witness the unfavourable example of the Midland chainmakers.who,men,women,and children, turn their homes into small working factories. It may be true that only these cottage employments have any chance where the goods find a ready sale, and can com- pete with any offered in the market, but if we are to have the conditions of the factory, it would be better to have the factory itself, and SAY nothing more about cottage industrier There is still however, considerable scope for those goods which combine the useful with fancy and &Bette work, for vrhich there is always some Measure of demand. Education, and schools of art, have also tended to withdraw people from the more humble callings, and instead of following villaee industries, they often try to live by more amlntieus means fer which very often nature has denied them the necessary talents. Many aspire to go on the stage, or become authors, or follow some or other profession, with the result that large num- bers go to swell the vast army of unhappy incompetents, who, it was said with some truth many years ago, would be the outcome of higher education and schools of art. The popularity of the Scottish ballad " Annie Laurie, is no doubt lame'c due to the music to which it was set by Lady J. Scott Spottis wood, whose death at an advanced age has recently taken place. The third verse which is considered by many people to be the best, was also writton by this lady, the other two being attributed to Douglas of Finglaad. There seems to be no doubt that the " Annie" to whom the lines were addressed was a daughter of Sir Robert Laurie, Bart, and that she was born in 1682 at Maxwelton House. Traditon says that she was a very beautiful girl, and that young Douglas was in love with her, but she nvisried a pros- perous prosaic country laird with whom she lived "happily ever afterwards." The London Scottish Societies have just oreanized a cleat benefit conceit in honour of Miss Jessie Maclarh- lan, who is unrivalled in her exquisite rendering of the popular Scottish ballads. Miss M'lntyre is, of course, one of the most accomplished singers of the day,but Miss Maclachlan is often regarded as the pima donna of Sootland, from 13er intimate acquaintance and rendering of its national music. She cornea fr sin the west of Scot- land, and scered her first great success in Glasgow, with- out previously undergoing any special training. She is well known in Leaden, appearing always at the concerte of the Gaelic Society, and she has done much to encour- age tte study of ancient Gaelic scnga and airs. Miss Maclacblan is shortly leaving on a tour in the United , Slates, and the Co'onies, where she will be much appre- ciated by the large Scottish element which is everywhere to be found in those distant parte. s Every year a new fashion in trains is brought forward by leading modistes. Last spring we had the train transparent, which by the way, proved anything but a tuccess ; this year we haven the train bung from the waist, instead of the shoulders. With a deep pointed corsage, the train from the waist is undoubsedly most becoming, as was evidenced over and over again, at the recent Drawing Room. Sleeves remEin quite tight-fitting, with or without the epaulette, real or simulated. They are still worn very long, and cut in a point, over the hand. A. few bleeves lately en evidence, have a puff introduced at the elbow, and another At the top, which, leoks as thoueh reaction had get in. But it is early yet to say whether the tight sleeve is doomed. Possibly it will last out the summer. " MADGE."

TREASURER.

CLAIM AGAINST A FOOTBALL CLUB

Interesting Case at Warrington.

At the Warringt7n County Court last Thursday before his Honour Judge Gwynne-James, Frank Tayler Harrop, late secretary of the Lymm Football Club, claimed the sum of £s4s 10d due to him as per the balance sheet from William Mullard the treasurer, money expended by him on account of the club.—Mr. Graham, of Manchester, appeared for the plaintiff, and the deienciant was repre- sented by Mr. A. Browne. Mr. Graham, in opening the cage, said that the plain- tiff, during the season 1898-99 acted as secitetary for the Lytnm Association Football Club and the defendant acted as treasurer. During the course of the season the plaintiff expended various sums of money ie diichargicg his duties, for stamps, telegrams,fees paid to the League Ste. certain of which were paid back to him. Towards the end of the season he sent his account showing the balance still owing to him from time to time.an shortly before the general meeting the defendant requested him to send another account in. An account was sent to the defendant showing a balance ot £5 4s 84cl. The defend- ant prepared a balance sheet which he submitted at a meeting called on the 17rle April. and in that balance sheet was an item of £5 41 10d, expenses of st cretarv. This was nearly the amount of the claim sent in by the plaintiff. After the claim was sent in the secretary found he had made a mistake. There was only 11 ' difference between the amount on the balance sheet and that of the claim. It was shown on the balance sheet that there was a email balance in hand after making the payments. When the general meeting was held the , defendant read out the statement and plaintiff called I his attention to the fact that he had not received the money. Defendant said he would attend to the matter and let him have the money in a day or two. From that time plaintiff did not receive a farthing. The bal- ance sheet was audited and found correct.

Frank Taylor Harrop, the plaintiff, then gave evidence. Re said he had not received the amount claimed. He was present at the meeting when the balance sheet was read out, and told the treasurer that the accsunt was not paid. Defendant said he WOUld send him the money.

By Mr. Browne : He had been shown the receipts (produced) which he had given for the amounts rEceived by him, totalling £5 4310 d, which, however,had nothing to do with the claim.

His Honour (to plaigtiff) : Have you any documents allowing how the amount was made up ? Plaintiff produced a book containing a statement of how the money was expended. _ _

His Honour-; What-ynu are asking for is a sum other than this, which curiously enough makes up £5 odd.

Plaiatiff : Yes sir. _ In answer to his Honour, plaintiff said that he sent in an account before the audit took place.

His Honour said that the position was a somewhat curious one. A receipt had been given for £5 43 101 which undoubtedly defendant had received, which, ir; turned out had nothing to do with the claim. Prima facie the plaintiff had expended money b yond that. If Mr Browne did not dispute it then the question was whether the plaintiff could recover the amount. No evidence bad been given beyond that there was a small balance in hand, that the club had received any money which they could pay. Mr. Graham said that on no occasion was it sug gested--- His Honour: I cannot go into that. Mr. Browne: The committee have refused to pass the bill. His Honour asked to see the rules of the club.

Mr. Browne: There are no rules except those of the Manchester lengue ani that does not affect the ems at all.

His Honour : I think it does; because the club is affiliated with the League. _

Mr. Browne sa'd tbe—re was no rule with reference to the passing of the accounts by the commitee. His Honour: The treasurer has money which has to be expended for the purpose of peyment of the debts of the society. If it is proved to my satisfaction that the money is due from the soc'ety it is payable out of the money held by the treasurer. Mr. Browne: Sappos;ng the Committee would not pass the account. His Honour said the Club had no roles. If they ha. i a rule that accounts were not to be paid until Dassed by the committee the treast•rer could not pay Vl* money. Mr. Browne : We are sued for money mentioned in the balance sheet.

His Honour (to plaintiff) : What you are suing for is not included in the balance sheet. Mr. Browne said that it was stated in the claim " as per the balance sec t of the Lymai football Club." STEVE, THE OUTLANDER.

BY ARTHUR LAYCOCK Author of "The Young Gaffer," "Seaside Sketches,7

"Lancashire Sketches," &c.

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS : Steve Hart, a factory operative, in love with Ella Rayburn, another factory hand. is compelled by the maliciousness of young Cartleigh, the factory manager, to leave his post. He determines to go to South Africa. Cartleigh intends, meanwhile, to win Ella from him. As scon as Hart has gone Cartleigh lays his plans, and seeks Ella's company in and out of work hours. By his disgraceful course of life he has alienated his parents, but, spurred by his pursuit of Ella, he determines to reform out- w-"iv so as not to be cast off by them.

===== 0002601_19000323_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER VII.

Left to himself Ralph fell into contemplative mood and reviewed his position.

"Perhaps it will be best for me to remain here a bit longer. There's my charmer in the weaving- shed, and I must see her. Then I must make my financial position stronger. The guv'nor's cut me out of his will, that I know. Well, that's no great calamity. His thousands would be little good to me if they made me as miserable as he is. If I'd an odd thousand I believe I would clear out and emulate that aspiring piecer, Steve, the Outlander I Ah! and that reminds me, I must be on my best behaviour for • a time. If the griv'nor turns out nasty and packs me off, and my pranks get talked about here, the dear little girl would doubtless be very much afraid of the black sheep of the family-. tip to now I fancy she regards me as most folks do—as the prospective heir to the Cartleigh riches. lam to her the factory-master's son, the superior person.' I must maintain that role. The villain of the piece' must be on his best behaviour and *mulate the good young man." So Ralph resolved that in the immediate future his conduct should be irreproachable, or, at least, as exemplary as he could ever expect it to be. He would frequent the Rose and Crown less. There was quite as snug a pub in Oldham that he knew of, and there he could sojourn and transact his business with the coterie of betting men with whom he had long been associated. He would also taboo the local coursing ground for a period; there was plenty of sport at the other grounds further away. Barrett, too, must not visit Dalesworth so often. He was not respectable-looking at his best; besides, the free-and-easy way he had addressed factory girls in the district, when he had chanced to encounter them in his walks with RalV_-.; had caused certain of the men folk to threaten pZAonal violence to both the factory-master's son and his companion. or oilier good reasons, also, Barrett must be kept away. "I'll ingratiate myself with pa and ma," said Ralph. "I'll attend church every Sunday and hear my beloved brother preach; I'll turn up punctually at work—horrid word I—every Monday morning; in short, I'll reform—perhaps ! " For he had considerable doubts as to the success of his efforts of reform.

His plan of campaign for securing the favour of Ella Rayburn was carefully considered and elaborated with the skill of a man well acquainted with human nature, particularly feminine human nature.

He perceived that too persistent attention to Ella Rayburn at this early stage would be impolitic, as the girl would doubtless resent his advances while she yet thought of her absent lover. A few weeks of complete loneliness would have due effect on her. She would yearn for company, for sympathetic society; she would fall permanently into the discontented state of mind which was manifested so plainly for a few moments on that Saturday night when the thought of her neglected, forlorn condition distressed her so much. _ _ _

_ So Ralph Cartleigh surmised, and, having such thorough knowledge of the feminine chttracter, he believed his surmises would be fulfilled to the letter. It was his boast that he knew as much of womankind as any fellow of his age. "And I'll know more of Ella Rayburn in six months than that fellow Hart would know in six years," he declared.

1- But he- would proceed with caution and so make more sure of his ground. He would see the pretty weaver lass at her work occasionally, but for a time would not even chat with her as heretofore. He would keep her well under view and see how isola- tion and loneliness affected her. Then at the ' right time he would come to her and be received , gladly. A cunning, clever, far-seeing plotter was he. Six weeks had elapsed since Stephen Hart left Dalesworth. At the end of this period Ella Rayburn received a letter from him, wherein he described the long voyage to the Cape, the life aboard ship, the people Whom he had encountered, the sights he had seen, with a brief account of his doings ashore since arriving in South Africa. Of his prospects and of his plans for the future he said but little ; indeed, in this respect, the letter was most disappointing to the recipient. No mention of gold or diamonds at all. Steve, how- ever, was confident and hopeful as ever. When it was known at the mill that Ella had received news from her lover she was questioned much by her workmates, and soon Steve's letter was the general topic of conversation. I -Ben, the warehouseman, encountering Ralph, and having a sly suspicion, strengthened by -his last conversation with the manager, that he was interested in such gossip, began: I "I've heard at Steve Hart's landed at yon out- landish Afriky country, Mestur Ralph." I "Indeed 1" exclaimed the young man, asiuming an interested air. I "Yes, his lass yon has got a letter from him this mornin', an' • though she doesn't tell all 'at's in it —which isn't likely considerin' that it may be 'sandwiched with love-talk—still, she's told enough to make one understond 'at he's not shovellin' up soverins yet." "Time enough for that," said the manager. "He's scarcely landed yet, you know." "No; but as soon as he set foot ashore he ought to have picked a bit of gowd up, I should think, for it's theer on th' say-shore, I understond." "How do you make that out?" I ((Well," replied Ben, with Conscious pride, "I've bin readin' up a bit about Afriky since last I talked with you when you chaffed me about knowin' so little about outlandish forrin parts, an' I've come across th' words 'Afric's gowden strand.' That sounds reet enough, doesn't it ? " " Ok, but that is but 'a poetic view, merely figlirative language. You mustn't take that as literal fact, Ben." "Then who can one believe?" cried Ben, in amazement. "Who can one believe if not parsons se poets? I thowt they were both truthful sort o' folks." I Fareons were anathema to Ralph. He at once turned the conversation in another direction. "So the letter is disappointing, you think, Ben P" "Well, it seems so to me. But I dar' say t' other part 'at th' lass has no' told about, th' love-talk an' sich like; will be all right an' encouragin' enough for her. Love-sick folk can be satisfied wi' so little. But wait till they're wed, they'll see how badly off they are wi' nowt but love-talk. Fine words dunno butter no parsnips; they'll want summat more substantial then, summit with some weft in it. They'll want gowd then to buy food for th' little 'uns yammerin' about 'em, for thoose little two-legged conse- quences come in th' way of all weddin's. Yes, when they get to that pitch they'll happen be glad 0* silver or copper, never name gowd. I know, I'm wed mysel'. "You are a sceptic, Ben, without a particle of poetry in your soul." - "Au' a good job, too, I think, if poetry's as unreliable as you say. An' to think 'at I got thoose lines fro' a hymn-book 1 It caps all! Th' next time I want to know owt about th' g'ography of Afriky I'll not go to a poet for th' information, I'll tell you," and Ben waddled off. "So she's received a letter from him, eh?' mused Ralph Cartleigh, as he sauntered towards the weaving-shed. "And it appears to be a rather disappointing epistle according to Ben's tale. I think I'll call to see her to-night. She should ' be getting in the right mood to appreciate my friendly overtures now." He passed into the weaving-shed and stood for a moment at the end of the alley where Ella was busy at her looms. The weaver lass looked up and caught the eye of the cunning fellow as he leered at her musingly and then passed on. That night Widow Rayburn was greatly surprised by a visit from the factory-master's son. At the time the busy dame was rattling her smoothing- iron over the white, shiny linen on the kitchen

table. The aavent or tne important visitor was the signal for sudden suspension of work. "Come formrd, kestur—Mestur Ralph," cried tthe widow, leaving her hot iron to scorch the lineu while she pulled out a chair and carefully due.cl the seat with her apron. _ _

"You must excuse th' house bein' upset, Mestur Cartleigh. It's bin washin' day, you see." She adroitly thrust out of sight the scorched linen and the smoothing-iron, then quickly set the housis in order. Ralph looked round for the chief object of his visit, and the widow, who was mean- while observing him as she bustled about, inter- preted the inquiring look. "Our Ella's just gone out. Did you want to see her?"

"Well, yes," returned Cart/eigh, "I happened to be passing, so thought I would pay you a visit. I've often told Ella that I should come to see you." He could not call her "Miss Rayburn" in her mother's presence. He knew, also, that the widow would be much flattered by this familiar reference to her daughter. "You've just done right to call and see us, Mestur Ralph, though we're nob'dy much to see, but all dacent folks are welcome here, I say. It's seldom 'at we're honoured with gentlefolks comin' a-seem' us."

She fussed and bustled around, clearing away every vestige of the "washing," which a few moments before appeared to litter the whole place. "Our Ella will be back directly, I expect. She's just gone to th' post-office with a letter. Eh, these letters, Mestur Ralph! She got one this Irnin' that she's bin expectin' for weeks, an` now 'at it's come nowell do but she must answer it at once."

"P'erhaps it was a letter from some particular friend ? " observed Cartleigh, seeking to draw the widow on to make further disclosures.

"Well, it was, in a way," she answered. "It was from Steve Hart, I may tell you. You'll know him well enough, but you'll happen not know 'at him an' our Ella's bin keepin' comp'ny." "I have heard something of it," he remarked, in a tone of ill-concealed displeasure. "Well, I don't know as there's anythin' much in it," said the widow, significantly. "Young folks keep comp'ny nowadays without much ides of owt comin' of it. Our Ella reckons to have a bit of a likin' for Steve sometimes, an' then again at other times she doesn't seem to know her own mind on th' matter."

Mrs. Rayburn now seated herself in her "couch cheer" opposite her visitor, and seemed disposed to chat confidentially. . _

"It isn't for me to meddle with our Ella's affairs, Iltiestur Ralph, but I must say that if I'd my way she'd have nowt no moor to do with Steve Hart. I• tell her many a time 'at she owt to do better for hersel' nor wed a ord'nary fact'ry lad; not as I despise fact'ry folk at all. Nor I wouldn't mind if Steve had made a dacent position for hissel', but to bother with a chap that's five- an'-twenty year owd an' has no', I'll be bound, five-an' twenty pound in all th' world to bless hissel' with, well, to bother with such-like is silly work, I say." After this lengthy speech she paused for breath, then, stroking out the creases from her white apron and looking up at her vis-à-vis occasionally, she resumed:

"She's fit for someone better nor a fact'ry lad, is our Ella, though it's me as says it, that p'raps oughtn't to. But I say it again, for I don't want my lass to throw hersel' away on some'dy as can- not give her a better position in life nor I've bin able to give her." Hearing this Ralph Cartleigh looked keenly at the woman to see if he could discover whether she had such as he in mind as a likely suitor for her daughter's hand. Widow Rayburn, however, now kept her face averted, directing her gaze on her plump hands still mechanically moving over her lap. p'raps not right," she proceeded, "that one should wish to have their child in a diff'rent position in life to what they've allus bin brought up to; but every mother wants to do th' best for her dowter an'- Hulloa, here she is 1-"

Any further confidences she might have been dis- posed to entrust her visitor with were now put aside as Ella entered the house.

The girl uttered an exclamation of surprise when Ralph Cartleigh rose to greet her. "Good evening, Miss Rayburn. kept my resolve." Any annoyance Ella might have felt was instantly dispersed by his cordial speech and amiable, courteous manner., He gallantly offered his chair to her and drew forward' another, and side by side they sat together. "There now, you look a gradely nice couple," the widow exclaimed, admiringly. "You're just like a couple of-" "Mother 1" cried the girl, reprovingly. Mrs. Rayburn discreetly withdrew and they were left alone. For long they chatted together in an easy, careless fashion, Ella forgetting all else except that she was feeling supremely happy under the admiring gaze of the factory-master's son. Though 'twas but one short hour since she wrote her letter to Steve, he was quite absent from her thought now. Every moment Ralph Cartleigh felt that he was gaining more complete mastery over her. 'She was as pliable in his skilful hands as he could wish, and without effort he moulded her to his will. When he suggested a walk through the fields, she willingly concurred. Far from being abashed in his presence, as she had been on the last occasion, she now felt quite •at ease, and chatted naturally and 'vivaciously with him.

You see, I have

"A complete victory," said Ralph Cartleigh, con.' gratulating himself on his success. When they parted company two hours later, each declared that they had passed an enjoyable time together, and when Ralph proposed that Ella should accompany him to Buxton on the" Wakes" trip, she was half inclined to fall in with his wish.

• But I'll not promise to-night, Mr. Cartleigh,* she said at parting. "I'll see next week." Frail, fickle Ella! When "next week" came, she had fully made up her mind on the matter, and promised to go with Cartleigh ; and thus became further entangled in the net he had spread out to ensnare her

CHAPTER VIII.

The annual weekly summer holiday, or "Wakes " as it is locally termed, was the red-letter week of the year to Dalesworth people. It was looked forward to with undiminished eager- ness by the young folks, and, as the happy time drew nigh, much speculation was rife in factory, mine, and workshop as to how, and where the holiday should be spent. Some would plan a whole week's visit to the seaside, others would be content with a week-end at such places, others again, with lighter purses, would patronise the day excursion. Ella Rayburn was of the latter class. To sport at the seaside for a full week would have delighted her greatly; but as she and her mother spent a goodly portion of their income on dress and personal adornment, the amount available for holidays was in consequence small.

Ella preferred to exhibit her finery to her imme- diate neighbours, rather than display it on the pier at Blackpool, where, however splendid her attire might be, she would assuredly be outmatched by scores of the wealthy ladies who frequent that fashionable over-sea promenade. When she met Ralph Cartleigh at Dalesworth Station at 8 a. m. on Thursday of Wakes-week, she was attired in a tasteful pink gown, neat-fitting and stylish, which shewed off her trim figure to perfection. The soft warm colour faintly tinged her usually pale cheeks, and the delicate, peach-like bloom was very becoming. Cartleigh decla‘red that she was "charming—a veritable blushing Juno." The journey to Buxton was exceedingly pleasant, and the time passed merrily with the twain. Ralph was in excellent spirits, and made himself especially pleasing to his companion, who, on her part, re- sponded gaily to his humour. The greater part of the day was spent in visiting the show places of Buxton. and promenading about the town. Late in the afternoon Cartleigh suggested a drive into the country. Ella pointed out that their train was timed to return act seven o'clock. "Oh, that's all right," said the young man. "We're not particular to an hour or so." "But I would like to go back by that train; it will be quite late enough," she said. "Very well. But we can manage to have a run round, and still be back in good time." Re had no intention, however, of returning in *tine to catch the excursion train.

They stepped into a landau, and Oartleigh • -•acructed the driver to proceed to a • village some miles distant. As the vehicle bowled along the I?zet-scented country lanes and through the shady avenues of mighty trees, Cartleigh, who had been facing his fair companion, seated himself at her side.

"You are enjoying this very much; now, aren't you ? " "Oh, yes, I am," she replied, enthusiasticalli Tt i<l glorious. But we mustn't miss our train."

11".1.77nr—Ideed," he returned, in mock seriousness. , e "That would be a dreadful calamity:, wouldn't it? ; We should have to charter a special. I'm sure . they'd oblige us with one." i She laughed heartily at his badinage. ; ift "Besides, if we couldn't get back, the fair 1 excursionist wouldn't be afraid of being left wit ; g me, would she ? " _ ,1

lie passed his arm round her waist as he glanced slyly into her face. She tried to release herself, but in vain; and after feeble protestation, playfully scolded him for his forwardness.

Suddenly, as the carriage swept through a dark avenue, the young man bent over her and imprinted a passionate kiss on her cheek. This roused her to violent anger, and, in an agitated, tremulous voice, she expostulated with him; and, calling to the driver, she peremptorily ordered him to turn back. The fellow, not in the least disconcerted by the incident, smiled at the girl's protest, and drove on. "I'll jump out of the carriage if he doesn't turn back at once!" cried Ella, now flashing pre from her moist eyes. Cartleigh quietly instructed the driver to return to Buxton, and sought by all the wiles of which he was possessed to appease the righteous wrath of his angry companion. But the girl was obdurate and sullen, and, for a bine, was totally impervious to the advances made towards her by the wily Ralph. When the town was reached, they stepped from the vehicle, and Cartleigh suggested that they should have tea.

"I want nothing," said Ella "except to be left alone. Go away from me, or I shall strike you!" "Oh, she's a vixen," said Cartleigh to himself. “I must be wary—of her; but she'll not beat me. I've seen her kind before."

A church clock near by was striking the hour of six.

"Come now, we must have something to eat," he said, coaxingly. "It is an hour yet to train-time. I'm famished, and I'm sure you are." They came to a cosy-looking tea-room, and the clink of cups and saucers caused Ella to turn her head aside and glance into the place. "Come, we'll go in here!" said Cartleigh, and before she had time to remonstrate, he had led her inside.

A cup of fragrant tea, with dainty tit-bits, had a most soothing effect on the ruffled girl. She allowed herself to be drawn into conversation again, and, in a cautious, sly way, Cartleigh chaffed her con- siderably. The return journey was not as enjoyable to the couple as the outward one. Ella relapsed into a sullen mood again, and her companion rebuked himself for his impetuosity and lack of discreetness. Nevertheless, he had such confidence in his own powers that he believed he could soon recover the ground he had temporarily lost. As he left her this night, he assumed a contrite and humble demeanour, expressing regret for his unfortunate slip. "It was foolish of me, Miss Rayburn," he said; "and I really don't know why I did it. I must have been---ah intoxicated, yes, intoxicated with love • but, hang it t anyhow, I couldn't help myself; I really couldn't." Ella listened to him in silence. 'All her protests had been made; she wouldn't repeat them. But she was determined not to run further risks.

"You'll forgive me, Miss Rayburn, now, won't ycT ? " - -

"Yes, I will, but—l shan't go out with you agaivi, Mr. Cartleigh." "What! Not again ? " "No; never!"

Her tone was convincing, but Ralph Cartleigh knew her frailty better than she herself did, and the words fell lightly on his ears. That was a sorrowful night for Ella. Long after she had retired she lay awake gazing out at the stars, with distracting thoughts harrowing her fickle heart. Visions of the bright morning passed before her; the merry ride on the train to Buxton, in company with her vivacious companion, the fair scenes of Derbyshire, the happy outing culminating in that unfortunate country drive. Then the stinging; stabbing thought that Ralph Ckrtleigh had kissed her, and she was the girl who had pledged her love to another—that other far away across the sea suffering and toiling for her—for her who had given herself up to this day's horror! Her better nature rebelled against the falsity. sobbiug. in distraction, she vowed that never again

would he compromise 'herself. She would be true to Steve. What though she would be lonely? What mattered it that she would be subjected to :he taunts of the girls? She would not heed them. 3he would think of Steve, and for his sake would 3uffer a little. Oh, what would Steve think if he knew that that man, above all others, had assumed such power over her? Sleep, welcome sleep, with sweet oblivion came to her release, and when morn broke the drowsy girl, awakening at her mother's call, dimly recalled the incidents of the previous day, and felt that she had been troubled with a "bad dream." And' soon the "bad dream" passed from her memory, for Ella's was not the mind to store up unpleasant recollections.

In the old monarchical days before the Revolution, the sessions of the old Chamber were dull and prosy affairs. One day, it is related, a noble count was trying to make a speech, and a very prosy speech it was, while all the other members were either chatting or resting. Pre- sently the president of the body rapped slightly With his baton. "If those gentlemen who are talking to each other," said he, "would kindly make no more noise than those gentlemen who -axe snoring, it would be much appreciated by those gentlemen who are trying to listen to the peaker !" How TIIE CHINESE JOKE.—A Chinese gentleman thinks it beneath his dignity to manufacture his "4.11 witticisms. When he goes into society, he carries into his pocket a packet of prepared witti- i.eisms and repartees. When conversation flags, and 'e Perceives an opportunity of saying something brilliant, he draws a humorous remark from the . toll of his packet and hands it to his neighbour With great gravity. The latter as gravely reads and selecting from his bundle of repartees the °Tie which is appropriate, returns it with a bow to the original joker. The two then smile in a courteous and undemonstrative way, and resume their conversation, feeling they have acquitted themselves with conspicuous brilliancy. A MILITARY MARVEL.—He was an old soldier— in both senses of the phrase—and, seated in the Parlour of the village inn, was favouring the assembled company with reminiscences of the battlefield. "I remember," he remarked, after a hearty pull at the pewter, "being at Talavera in 54. Ah ! there was pretty hot work that day, lads,•l can tell you. The Roosians had got two batteries playin' on our battalion as we advanced at the double, an' the balls were fallin' all round us like hail. The captain of my company was leadin' us, sword in hand, an' cheerin' on to charge the batteries, when a ball knocked his head clean off his shoulders. I reached him in less than a minute, and as he fell he looked towards me an' said, 'Corporal, bury me just where I stand ; ' and we did it that night, after taking the battery, With all funeral honours possible under the circum- stances." bouBLE-CHARG ED.—An old sportsman named eorge Shaffer had been out shooting, and was r'iling home with his bag empty, and weary, when ',stopped at a tl-house for a moment's rest. • here's a fine lot of ducks back here in the pond," he to the toll-gate keeper. NOTlCE.—Solvers who become entitled to a prize in this comp3tition can, if they desire, have any book on the game of Draughts equal to the value of ss. instead of cash prizo, by notifying the Draughts Editor.

In connection with the above competition we offer a monthly prize of 5s to the competitor who succeeds in placing hiais,=lf at the head of the Solvers' List at the end of the month. Where the prize is awarded the winnPrs' score is cancelled, while the remaining competitors' points go towards the next months competition. The winner may re- enter the tourney it he chooses, commencing again at the bottom of the list. Points are awarded as follows : Two for the correct solution of a prob- lem ; two for correcting the author's terms, one for showing a dual solution or a dual key-move, and one for showing a dual continuation at any stage in the solution.

NEWS OF THE GAME.

1 T. - - ISIT CF THE ENGLISH CHAMPION TO - WARRINGTON. On Monday and Tueday last Willie Gardner, the English champion, and champion Blindfold Draughts-Player of the world, gave an exhibition of scientific draughts playing in Cairo-st. schools. On Monday evening Mr Gardner gave an exhibition of simultaneous blindfold playing, a feat which popularises Mr Gardner wherever he goes. Twelve of the strongest local players were selected to do battle with the champion, but the result was a large majority for the single player. Mr F Dunne kindly officiated as caller. Mr Gardner frequently elicited applause by running up the moves in several of the games during the course of the even- ing, a performance which was fully appreciated by players and spentators alike. I,4IARONGTON ASSOCIATION OF BANDS.

Mr T J Dawn ).21.eside at the annual meeting of this Asseciation held iu the e, ranee Chameer at the T iwn - Had oe mad y hag. Anaenge: those &leo present were Councillnr Sm rst, and;Mr J Taylor, with Mr B etha' 1 Wad • worth (hon seer. lAry, • AN N 17AL REPORT. Tee Hon. Secretary's replle; 'read as : —la presenting' the followine reryoite I bee' corially to thank the biedsm ,n of Warrinetou 4.7 w ln wllich thy have worked for the benefit of otk, townspeople. Daring this nest year there have been 51 .lerforalences, as against 145 'given last yeer. If a baud La not ap- peared on the sti.nd on tee date app)inted. to cause has this year always been found in th wearhertart) not (as, I believe, has been the case in some former yeao) because a bandmaster c Juld not get Les men together. The number of bands taking part in th • work of the Beeson has been eleven, as rgaireat nine last yea r,but one of them, Messrs. Monks, Hafl aed Co.'s, only gave what mi_eht be termed an open rehearsal on waste ground near S. Lake's Church, Liverpool-road, and received no pay for it. The performance seemed to be mcch en joyed by th large number of people preseet. Toe greet bulk of the work has fallen on the Volunteer, Borough, League of the Cross, ;led St. Paul's Bands, who have amone them liven 38 performance, leaving 13 for the remaiteee bands. At the beginning of the season we were allowed to else an increase of 63 per man for a Sunday performance, bet the grant of 61. per man is still the full sum received for a week-dey performance The adeitional sixpence for Sunday duty has brought our last year's credit balance of £l5 7s 3.1 down to £7 Os id, as will be seen from the honorary treaserer's statement. At this rate I am afraid that the close of next season will find us slightly in debt, unless, indeed, the weath-r assists our finances, by putting a stop to our perform- ances, which I hope will not be the case. An expri- mont has been tried this year, mainly on the strengtn of a suggestion which our President made in his address last year. I refer to the ivine of performances in some of the open spaces of the town. Ten such perform- ' ances have been given with excellent results, and have apparently given great satisfaction to the residents of the neighbourhood. The places visited have been Howley Recreation Ground, Orford-lane fair ground, open space in Be wsey-road,epen space at Sankey Bridges, and open space at the top of Lovely-lane. These performances have more tl an once been patronizA by the town councillors for the ward, and have brought forth their warmest commendations. Though it is an extra sacrifice on he part of the players often in the distances to be travelled, the carriage of music stands, bundles of music, &c., and also in the extra work required of their ifiatruments at the close of a perform- ance, because of dust,l believe the men are quite willing to make these sacrifices again in the interests of the public. Alter alluding to the loss sustained 'op the death of Councillor Dr. Caunell be concluded : We are age:in looking forward to another season of good work,and feel confident that we shall receive the loyal support of all bandsmen in endeavouring to cater for the enj ryrnent and education of the people of our own good old town. (Applause.)

THE BALANCE SHEET. The balance sheet showed that the receipts totalled to £75 us. 10. Tha year commenced with a balance in hand of £l5 7t, 5 I and the bank interest amounted to 3s 81. Toe only other item of income was a con ribu- tion of £6O from the Warrington Corporation. The amount paid to bands was £67 176 61, leaving a balance in hand of £7 Os I.d.

SUGGESTIONS BY THE PRESIDENT. The PRESIDENT said it app ,are i from the reports that the aff:iirs of the association were in a fairly satisfactory condition. The only actual item of receipt was £6O from the Corporation, while during the year they had spent £67 17s 61. Be was pleased to note that they had been able to give more public performances than in the pre- vious year. That was a good sign. Still he thought it was a matter for some regret that the number of bands in connection with the association was not so large at the present time as a few years ago. He did not actually know how to account for that, but there was, he thought, an undoubted fallinf.s, off in mutical eathusiasm in the town. He suggested last year that it might be dug to football, cycling, &c., and very likely that had a ame- , thing to do with it. It seetnod to him that they must make the best of things as they had found them,_, and their duty was to make what buds they had as efficient as they possibly could be, and try to re-awaken that interest which for tho moment seemed to be more or less dormant. He was glad to notice that the tide had turned in some degree, and that on Monday night a new band was admitted into the asanciati'yl. He hoped that in time they would be as strong as they were same four or five years ago. It was gratifying to know that all bands belonging to the association were in a sound and healthy condition, and, as Colonel Baden-Powell would say " were going streng." (Hear, hear.) Be did not think personally that the Warrington public ever heard better music in the Park than that supplied last year. He thought it was rather above the average. He considered that all the bands that took part in the public performances showed some signs of progress,and that was satisfac'ory. With regard to the monetary grant from the Corpora- tion, it was his opinion, and had been for some time, that the amount given was not sufficient. He thought the band,men who gave their time and skill for the recreatian of their fellow townsmen were entitled to a fair payment for their services. (Hear, hear.) At present they were paid 61 for each performance. He thounht it ought to be a shilling at the very least. He did not know very much about what was done in other towns, but in Liverpool the bandsmen were paid better than in Warrineton. Tien there was another matter. It had been mentioned to him several times how much wind- guards or shutters were needed for the stand in the Park. He woold suggest that four be erec'ed and tried as an experiment. In conclusion he also alluded to the necessity of ligla' ing the stand. On several occasions it was so dark that the players were unable to see their music. He would suggest that as the Corporation were laying electric cahies that a wire be run to the band stand. He thought the matters he had mentioned would receive careful consideration if placed before the Town Hall, Park and Baths Com- mittee. The SECRETARY was instructed to ask the Town Hall, Park, and Baths Committee to grant £9O for the ensuing year, and to carry out the other suggestions of the Pres- ident.

Wins

ELECTION OF PRESIDENT AND VICE-PRESIDENTS. Mr. DOWN wai unanimously re-elected president and the following were elected vice-presidents : .The Mayor (Alderman It pberts), Messrs. A H Crosfield and R A Naylor, Aldermen Bolton and Wright, Coun- cillors Heaton, Smethurst, F W Monks,and the members of the Town Hall. Park and Baths Committee. TREASURER AND SECRETARY. Mr. JOHN FAIRHURST was re-elected hon. treasurer ; Mr. IS Wadsworth hen. secretary. PRESENTATION TO ME. J. TAYLOR. The PRESIDENT, On behalf of the association, said he had the greatest pleasure in asking Mr. Taylor's accept- ance of a case of cutlery and a breakfast cruet as a small token of the very high esteem which the members of the association had for him. (Hear, hear.) They could not forget that he was their first secretary, and upon his shoulders to a great extent fell the work of the organisation and arrangement of the affairs of the ' association. The work could not have been better done, and they were greatly indebted to Mr. Taylor. (Hear, bear.) He did his work always in such a quiet and un- ostentatious way that peonle did not realise thoroughly the good he had done in the town. In addition to his work for the association, Mr. Taylor had done some admirable work in connection with the Workingmen's Infirmary C3ommittee acid the Nursing Association. Wherever there was good work to be done Mr. Taylor was always well to the front but did it in such a quiet way that it was not everybody who knew it. The mem- bers of the association knew, and he (as president) asked him to accept the presents as a token of their respect and esteem and in appreciation of his public work. (Apnlause.) The presents had been subscribed for by the League of the Cross band, the Buckley-street band, his (the President's band), the Volunteer band and St. Paul's band. t Applause.) Mr. TAYLOR said he could hardly find words to express his gratitude for the many kindnesses ho had received at their hands in the efforts he had put forth to further the interests of the association and music generally in the town. He hoped they would extend to Mr. Wads- worth the same good feeling, loyalty and help that they had extended to him. He had heard many expressions of thankfulness for the bands from workingmen who had been able to it in the park and enjoy their pipe and lis'en to the music. He considered that the i,anda- men who gave their tame for the benefit of the public made sacrifices to do so. He felt proud of his connec- tion with the aasociation and of the part he bad taken in I doing some little good on behalf of his fellow townsmen. I (Applause.)

Novel Claim Against the Bold Hall Estates Company.

At St. Helens County Court. on Wednesday, before his Trfr,nour Judge Shand, Emily Stevenson, of Clock Face, St. HelenS, brought a rather novel claim against the Bold Hall Estates (Jimpany, Mr. H. L. Riley appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr. Peace de- fended. Mr Riley sail that Mrs. Stevenson had a lodger named Christina Blow), who was killud at the Clock Vane Colliery, on October 10 She now claimed Lit) from the company for the expenses of Boose's funeral. The claim was for £9 6 but they agreed to take £7 fis 51 The deceased was a mem- ber of the Lancashire and Cheshire Miners' Perms- , nent Relief Society, which society had paid appli- cant £l5. She had entered into an agreement to 'pay POMO of the debts of the decease& which wou!d ab;orb the £l5. Mr. Peace, for the respaidents, said that the deceased was entitled to £5 from the relief society, and the other £lO had been paid specifically for the funeral expenses.—His Honour after hearing the argument and the evidence of Mr V Mawdesley, cashier of the colliery, awarded, plaintiff £2 6s 51.

Compensation for a Warrington Widow Aft..r the Workmen's Cempenee.tion Act, 1897, cams into force on August 6.1897, the employees of the Pearson and Knowles Coal one Iron Company agreed to form a mutual arrangement with their employers to be in substitution of the above Act, and to cover any accident that might occur to a workman, whether through his own negligence or not, and to enjoy all the benefits which are con- ferred by the Workmen's Comnensation Act. This arrangement has been carried oat in every case, both at their Wigan works and at Warrington. Last week, the Workmen' e Arbitration Committee met to transact their quarterly business and to award compensation for a fatal accident that occurred to one of their workmen namei John 'Owens, a labourer, who had met with his death 'while riding upon the front of a cane that was being conducted from the Dallam Works to the Bew;ey Works, and was knocked off by the jib, he not. being observed by the driver,and fell under the wheels The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death," and freed the Company from all blame or liability. Notwithstanding this, the firm was pre- pared to pay the full amount allowed by the Act, and the Committee therefore awarded compensation to the widow to the amount of £l5O. Smoking Concert at the Liberal Club. On Friday evening last week a smoking concert was held in the reading-room at the Liberal Club, and proved to be as successtul as any of the previ- ous ones. The great feature was the variety of instrumental music given Mr J Worrell occupied the chair in his usual efficient manner. Mr W Woods opened the concert by giving the song " Anchored," and then Mr GI Grounds delighted the members by the excellent manner in which be rendered an euphonium solo, " The death of Nelson." Mr J Chappell sang" Lucky Jim," Mr W Ford, jun., gave a cornet solo, "Queen of the earth," and later on, at the revest of the Chairtnan,repeated the same. Mr W Critchley sang "The white squall," and Mr T Davies gave a cor Anglais solo. This instrument was a novelty to the majority of the members present and the masterly manner in which the solo was given was greatly appreciated. Mr Davies also gave a solo on the hautboy, selections from the "Bohemian Girl," and Mr E Saunders a sketch en- titled" A matter of business," which caused great amusement. Mr L Lea sang "For old time't sake." and Mr Hope gave a pianofor:e s,lo, selections of Irish airs and a waltz. Mr J Chappell and Mr W 1 ' Critchley accompanied the songs,and Mr Hope some of the solos. A vote of thanks to the Chairman,pro- wed by Mr George Beard, and seconded by Mr Thomas Lecker,was carried. The concert concluded with the singing of the National Anthem. Volunteer Intelligence.

The battalion orders for the firzt Volunteer Batta- lion ) South Lancashire Regiment contain the following notifications :—The following N. Co Officers and men having proceeded ten route to Sluth:Africa,are thereby rendered suplrnumerary t the establishment of the battalien. No. 3253 Lance Sergeant G J Buckley, F Company, 4674 Private H Holloway, F Company, 4878 Private P Burke, E Company. 3764 Private W E Longmore, C Company, 4505 Private A Ashton, B Company.—The following extract from district orders, dated 19th February 1900 is published for information. "As it is pro- bable Volunteer Battalions may this year be re- quired to encamp for a longer period than usual and in localities where range facilities do not exist, Commanding Officers will commence the Musketry training of their men early, in order, as fat as pos- sible, to complete the course before proceeding to camp." in accordance with the above orders the drill season this year will commence with musketry parades,which it is requisite that all trained volun- teers should attend prior to shooting on the Range, as the course laid down is a new one. No volun- teer will be allowed to shoot, therefore, until he has attended at least three of these parades. Section Commanders and N.C.O's generally should, attend as many more as possible. These parades will count towards the prefitiency of those N.C.O's and men desirous of becoming Ist class volunteers.

ST. PATRICK'S DAY EAR L-E-S -T -0 W N

Virulent Speeches.

In conneeion with the Earlestown branch of the Irish N atioael League, St. Patrick's day was celebrated by a concert, at whice Dr. O'K mfe of St. Helens and Mr. J Pinker ten M.P. were present. The room was well filled with Irrahmen, and an erre ,•llent program no of songs was sunmitned by Messrs. R Kay, C Nary, J Moran, Madam B llogg and Miss B Reynolds. Councillor Dr. O'Keefe of S. Helens, who presided, slid he had much pleasure in b_iing present,and it was not that h bad the pleasure of s Ting such a fine body of Irishmen tege!her. He noticed as he looked amongst them that a great number of them were not weariag the 1 shamrock, but he had seen a number of their E .glish friends wearing it, and he coneiderei it an infringement of an Irishinan'e rights for them to de so. The sham- rock was the Irishmen's glorious right long before Great Britain WAS known. He did not necd tell them how. He dared say that England thought she was honouring them in allowing thorn to wear the shamrock. Instead, however, be thought that if there had been any conces- sion at all, it bad been mede by the Irish people to the English people in allowing them to wear it. (Hear, hear.) Tee Home Rule question bad been a very diffi- cult one, and various • had been the methods to obtain this concession from the British Government. He was a believer that they would get Home Rule, but he was also a believer that they should have .to get it by force of arms. (Applause.) They would net use a "Long Tom," nor the bayonet ; the weapon, that they must use, was Unity. Withow, this weapon in their hands they could not win it. They had the weapon and they had the men, and now they wanted the ammunition. Momey was what they wanted. (Hear, hear,) Mr Ridge moved " That we the Irish people in E 'ries town sympathise with the cause of self government fo- Ireland and view with satisfaction the reunion of thr Irish p arty and hope that the united efforts will be pros ductive of good in our mother country." Mr Flannigan see)ndeis and it was carried. Mr John Pinkerton, M P. said for his own part he lied no such compunction against anyone wearing the shamrock at all. (Hear, hear.) He thought that it was a very great tribute to the Irish soldiers that the Queen sh mid allow them to wear the shamrock. (Cheers.) He did not regard it as a con- cession. He did not think that when an Englishman. wore the shamrock he did it in any other sense than as an act of courtesy to the Irish. He WAS glad that Her Majesty in the evening of her reign had paid this great tribute to the Irish soldiers. (Cheers ) With regard to the war in South Africa he said he was sot in favour of it. Be never was. He believed that when we had succeeded in taking Pretoria we should not be a single inch nearer a solution than we were before we com- menced. He considered the war was got up by men like Cecil Rhodes to line their own pockets, and not in any way to benefit their own country. He had the pleasure of seeing Dr. Jemeson at Westminster Hall at the time of the trial in connection with the raid. He saw the man sitting near the Select Committee and as he did not know who it was, it immediately struck him that he had not enough intellieence to look after a goose- berry stall. (Interruption.) Dealing with the question of Unity, they were, he said, inclined to ask themselves why this qeestioa was not beereht up three years ago. The Irish in Great Britain had quite as much interest in this coestion as the people in Ireland had. Be regarded the Irish in England as the backbone of the Irish cause, their devotion was surprising. Even the second genera- tion, people who hai never se.na Ireland, were as loyal to the mother country as the natives, (Applause.) _

Votes of thanks tO th Cnairman and Mr. Pinkerton were duly accorded.

Spfrirnens of the Igreen" as well as clover and ether substitutes were generally displayed. At the Market shamrock revizld as MU3h as 81 and ls for a small bunch and in sons?, cases even more. Commanding Lancashire Volunteer Infantry Brigade. The following extract from the London Gaz-tte dated 28 la F.!bruary, 1900, Colonel W. de W. R. Thack well, C.B. (Honorary Major General, retired pay) retires from command of the Brigade. under Paragraph 111 Volunteer Regulations." Major General W. Thackwell, C. 8., in taking leave of the Brigade has kindly if39ll.et a farewell order, from which the fol- lowing extracts are published for information. 'My command of the Lanc..shire Volunteer Infantry Brigade havins! terminated I must express to the officers, non- commissioned officers and men not only of the bat- talions forming the present brigad 3 but also to those Cheshire battalions who for so m +.ny years were under my command as the combined County brigade my gratitude to them for the loyal and unwavering support that I have always received. Daring the 11 years of my command some 40,000 men have been in Camp and during that long time not a single instance of misbehaviour in t'ae town or neighbourhood has cieen brought to my notice. In bidding farewell I feel that I am parting with many friends and I shall always look back with pleasure to my association with the Brigade and I feel certain that the Battalions with which I have been connected will always uphold their high position among the best of Her Majer,y's great citizm army.- 512ned W. THACKWELL, Major General, C.B. 28:h Feb., 1900."

STOCKTON HEATH

WHITEFIELD TENNIS CLUB—The annual meeting of thi,, club was held the other evening at the residence of Mr. Beuchier, Mr Wil!rel Sklton presiding. Mr. E. England (hon. secretary) preserted his report for the year, which showed that the club continued to make satisfactory progress, the membership having reached 35 From the report ie aleo transpired that not only had tennis been indulged in but that the social desires of the Imembers had also been met, and as the members of the Iclub had co-operated with the officials ot the club in a hearty and praztical maener, these social gatherings had been carried to a succesifut issue and very much enjeyet Theee social gatherings took the form of a picnic, two garden parties, and a dance during the win- ter months. The championship of the club had been played for, and won by Mr. E Eagland, with Mr. G D]lan and Mr. M R Tomkinson as the runners-np in the order named. The ladies' handicap resulted in the prizes being divided between the Misses M and H B elm- lord and Miss Mabel E eeland. A match had alio been played with the Penketh Cycle and Lawn Tennis Club, which, after a very exciting and close game resulted in a win for Puaketh. The Hon. Treasurer (Mr. J W Bouchier) presented his report, which showed that the finances of the club were in a satisfactory condition, there being a balance in hand on the year's working. It was decided at the meeting to erect a pavilion, and OA committee was d:reettd to take the matter in hand. It was also agreed, owing to the numerous applications for membership and as it was felt that all could not be accommodated, to limit the membership to 40. The hon. treasurer and hon. secretary were re-appointed, Mr R Tomkinson appointed captain, and a committee, consist- ing of six ladies and six geitlernen, was also elected. A vote of thanks to the Ceairman and to Mr. Bouchier for the use of his house for the eveni • g brought a very successful meeting to a close.

ALLEGED EXTENSIVE THEFTS.

At the Warrington Police Court on Toursday before Alderman Sutton (in the chair) and Mr. Hepherd, Barnard Cohen, a canvasser, of 22, Barry-street, was charged with stealing some time between the 2nd Febru- ary and 3rd March, three overmentels value £7 103 03 the goo ds of Charles Paikin, of Mersey-street.—Mr. A Browne prosecuted and Mr. F. R. Roberts appeared on behalf of John Gibbon, of Mersey-street, who according to the statement below. was implicated in the ease.— Mr. Browne said that Mr. Paikin was a furniture dealer carrying on business in Merses-street and since July. 1899 the prisoner had been in his employ as a collector and traveller. From time to ti Ile he had said a large quantity of goods and the names of the persons to whom he said he had sold them were entered in the books. In consequence of whet 6udotintendent Monaghan heard on Satterday, Mr. Paikin Wad communicated with, and he visited three houses in Dalton Bank where prisoner alleged he had sold three over-mantels. Be found that there was not a single name correct and no overmantels had been sent to the houses. Superintendent Monaghan knew where the articles were and in consequence of the result of inquiries and certain communications being sent asking for payment of money, Mr Paikin consulted him (Mr. Browne), and a warrant was issued. When prisoner was arrested the previous morning it was be- lieved that only th:es overmantels were missing, but after further inquiries it was found that a Mr Gibbon. of Mersey-streec, had bought 2 oil paintings, 8 pictures, 2 overmentels, 3 table cloths, 3 bedsteads, 12 sheets, 11, quilts, and 2 shirts, from the prisoner within three weeks time. Mr. Gibbon had admi'ted that he knew Cohen was employed by Mr. Paikin. The value of the goods amounted to something like £l.B or £l9. Whether they should proceed against Gibbon or not was a question for consideration, and he asked for a remand until Saturday. Mr. Gibbon admit- ted buying other goods from the prisoner, and had given an undertaking to get them back again. He miebt say on behalf of the prisoner that he had done everything he could since apprehended to explain where the goods had gone to, and his client was not inclined to be hard upon him. They could not free him from every blame as he had taken the goods deliberately sad sold them to Mr Gibbon and given false names to Mr. Paikin or his clerk as having sold goods to them. It was a very ingenious fraud. Some of the geode were not found on Mr. Gibbon's premises but one of Mr. Gibbon's danehters was seen by Super ntendent Monaghan going along Academy-street with a bundle. Ile followed her and found she deposited the contents in a house on Town Hill under a hamper. This put Mr. Gibbon in a peculiar position,—The Chairman : Were the goods sold for the full value P—Mr. Browne: No sir sometimes not a sixth. Continuing Mr. Browne said that the overmantels which cost 30s. were sold for 13s. The pictures cost 5 guineas and were sold for £l. Prisoner was remanded till to day (Saturday), bail being refused.

MARCH 23RD, 1900.
