THE EXAM IN ER.

UPON one or two points the Where the Liberals Liberal party, divided as it Can do 'Good. is, can do effective national service at the present juncture. Upon two points It will have to be very decided. The first is that the mining interests in he Transvaal shall bear a substantial part of any indemnity taxation, and the second is that the mining influence shall not be allowed to rule the whole of the Transvaal here- after. Some dangers will attend any strong and and bold attitude upon either point. There are other interests in the Rand than British interests. There are German, French, and Dutch share- holders in the mines, and a good deal of pressure Will be brought to bear upon our Government to make the taxation on the gold industry as light as Possible, which means, of course, that a good deal of the cost of the war and the cost of maintaining an army of occupation will have, in that event, to fall upon uhe British people. The mines can well afford to pay double what they have been paying under the system which existed up to the time of the declaration of war. To put it in Plain words, the Rand mines ought to contribute in one way or another about six millions a year to the cost of the war, the occupation, and th 3 ordin- ary expenditure. The precise way we leave un- described. It will have to be determined by cir- cumstances. Ent there will be a prodigious expansion a 9 soon as the mines get into working order again, and for that taxation will have to be paid. Wbat will be the fate of the railways we do not know. They will be taken over, we should think, and worked on a more liberal system. An enormous immigration may he expected, and the railways will have to deal generously with the now Comers. Whether Johannesburg should be Wrecked or not, it is clear that it occupies a favourable site, and that thure must be the gold reef centre. The second point is equally Imp 3rtant, and it may be urged by means i of all the weapons which have done duty ' on the Government side during the war. If We have been endeavouring ta rescue the Trans- vaal tbf rom an oligarchy of Boers and Hollanders, we f e careful not to hand it over to an pligarchy o mining men chiefly Jaws and Germans. All the varied political interests in the Transvaal must be :represented, and a start made with a system which will effectually prevent the play of i,money ;as". a prominent factor. Qualifications for the new Irolksraad we shall set up in local affairs must not be on the property basis, and an electoral system must be so arranged that voting shall be secret :and that bribery shall be prevented. Unless we do these things we had better govern the country as a Crown Colony for some little time to come ; and that step may be regarded as wise under any cir- cumstances. The Boer population must be fairly represented, aid if Boer members are elected, and oven become Ministers, so much the better. Whatever may be done about police and arms, the political side must be free and unrestrained, Protected alike from money and violence. The sooner the Liberals can begin to shape their thoughts upon these subjects the better. But it should be done without rhetoric and with moder- ation and argument. Hysterics on the subject would only lead to disaster. We do not grant the Contention that the Rand has engined the war, but with all the more fervour can we contend that the Rand than not dominate the Transvaal, as a matter of course. Numbers will be on the side of the outlanders, hut if we protect freedom and equality we can do much to.moderate and check the growth of undesirable influences.

THE new Education Code Levelling down in is a revolution. It will be Education. a shock to all education- ists. It is the last outcome of the old Education authority. The present payments V) schools are rude upon a series of subjects,by which emulation is excited, and by which the more enterprising schools and teachers earn more money than the duller and apathetic schools. If reversing this be uot a levelling down, the term has no meaning. What educationists have always feared has been -something of this drastic, Procrustean characte;, but they hardly believe that Sir JOHN GORST "would sanction it He has, however, to defend 'what has been done, and we shall be curious to `see how he will do it. He has, invariably posed as the friend of education,who lamented the indiffer- -4113C8 of school managers and of the subscribers to the Voluntary schools. When the special grant 'was given to the Voluntary schools it was to help them in their struggle for existence. Icamediately the subscriptions began to fall it was tlavious that some of the Yoluntary schools most cease to be efficient, and come under the School Iloard system. A. few of them did so, and the 'Voluntary Associations were powerless to help they changed their plan of paying most niclley to such schools as :were suffering by direct ec)mpetition with large and efficient Board schools. The Education Department has had to meet a cusis, and it has done so in a characteristic way. It has lowered the flag of education by abolishing the s- ystem of earning grants by merit, except for °Tie or two subjects. ,There is no shortening of the i.irricultim in other respects. The subjects on Which grants were earned in proportion to merit Will :still be retained on the school course, but the payment will be made in a different Way. It will be calculated at so much per head according to attendance. The effect of this new rule, called the block grant, will he somewhat singular. It will give more per head all round than has ever been awarded be- fore. But the more efficient schools will receive

-- IRE

===== 0002601_19000323_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
ess,ancl the less efficient schools will receive more— that is, relatively to former earnings...lnfant schools will be awarded from 16s to 173 per head on aver- age Attendance, and other schools from 21s to 225. It works out as it was intended to do—in favour of Voluntary, schools. The Board schools will re- ceive 8d per head more than they have previously earned by grants according to merit. But the Voluntary schools, whose grant earnings have been low, will receive is 4d per head of the block grant, and probably no subscriptioDs will now be necessary. That is the levelling down process—or the levelling up process, hecause it is both according to the point of view from which we re- gard it—is to be applied to the two descriptions of schools. Board schools need not be so efficient, because the stimulus of grant earning will be re- moved, so that education is levelled down. The Voluntary schools will receive more money by an extra Bd. over the Board schools,and to this extent bat only financially, it is a levelling up. The sub- ject isbound to excite warm debate in the House of Commons. It is obviously framed in the interest of the Voluntary schools, and a bonus of 81, per head per scholar is given to the Beard schools to create the appearance of fairness. Tile change will entail a demand on the country f, •r an extra £200,000, but it is not going to give us an equiva- lent in improved education.

Sir William Lockhart was a prescient little general after the" Bobs" pattern, whose death, at the age of fifty-nine, this country will greatly de- plore. It is only necessary to recall the way in which he triumphed over serious difficulties in the Tirah expedition two years ago to recognise his talent. Ho had, moreover, a gre tt repatation, and among a native population that is especially valu- able. The days have long passed when we could rule India, or any considerable part of it, by force. Our position there, of course, largely depends upon racial differences, and the tactful superiority of the Administration in times of emergency. Mr. Savage Lander, in an article in the" North American Review" this month, ob- serves that in times of famine there is considera- ble danger of a rebellion. Probably there would be greater danger without the famine. It pro- duces an emergency in a time of excitement, but the situation is met, and the trouble passes. With sleek prosperity, a seditious Press, and a tlndency among the lower military and civil officers to be unnecessarily rough to those about them, it is quite probable that the native races might assert their indepenlence. Our source of security, how- ever, lies in the united Imperial Administration, and with sagacity and decision of character on the part of the heads of departments British authority in India may continue to work smoothly.

There are many men who must regret they did not suffer from consumption in their youth. It might seem grievous at the time, but it has been the making of nearly all the prominent statesmen of British birth in South Africa, and had they re- mained in England thay would probably have been village Hampdens. The list grows daily as the personal particulars come to hand. Everybody knows that Cecil Rhodes was a weakly under- graduate, and was sent to the Cape to preserve his life. lid looks robust enough to-day, but his prospect atone time was dewessing in the extreme. Sir Gordon Sprigg was another weakling. He would probably have had some inglorious prosper- ity had he remained at home. Mr Garratt, the editor of the Cape Times, who is credited with having inspired Sir Alfred Milner's policy, was a sub-editor on the Pall Mall Gazette, and promised to continue an able journalist, but one of the unknown and unappreciated guides of public opinion, and to have died in early life because ®f the delicacy of his constitution. Last, but not least, it has transpired that Sir GKlfrey Lagden. the British Commissioner in Basutoland, who has rendered such signal service to the Empire an I to civilisation by keeping the Basutos in check, was a clerk in the General Post Offiee. But for failing health he would have been there still—a small dignitary at St- Martin's-le- Grand, using his intellect privately to enable the authorities there to meet Mr. Henniker- Ileaton's conundrums in the House of Commons on Post Office administration. All these men fell sick, and had to leave their country, in the new and best sense of the term,for their country's good. All their colleagues individually saw nothing re- markable in their labours here. The occasion made their distinction. It revealed unthought of strength and unexpected capabilities. But with- out this stimulus to emigration they would prob- ably have been lost in the fierce competition here during their brief existence. It is a hint to the capable men whose scope is limited to seek fresh fields of conquest.

Warrington has been honoured with another request from the War Office for more Volunteers. We cannot say whether this is in consequence of the splendid set of men Warrington furnished in response to the last request, but the fact remains that another body like them is required. There is every reason to believe that the call will be promptly responded to. Less difficulty will be experienced in this direction than in the matter of equipping them. Where are the funds to come from ? The grant from the War Office is totally inadequate for the purpose. It is therefore probable that we shall have to fall back again on Sir Peter Walker's gift account. Then what about the field •glasses and insurance policies? The public subscriptions given for this object are nearly, if not quite, exhausted. Such difficulties must not daunt us. They must be overcome somehow. In years to come when the war drum has finished beating in South Africa, Warrington will look back with pride on the action of her volunteers, and it therefore behoves us to meet the responsibility now entailed in no grudging spirit.

===== 0002601_19000323_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON ARTS AND CRAFTS EXHIBTION.

The popularity of the Exhibition which was so marked at the beginning, promises to be fully main- tained r'ght up to the finish. During the past week the attendance has been even larger than in the previous ones. The proruoters very wisely decided to take advantage ot the patriotic celebration on St. Patrick's Lay by providing a special programme of the music of Old Ireland in honour of the Queen's gallant Irishmen. A large number of patriotic airs were played with taste and skill by the Probyn Ladies' orchestra, and some humorous pieces were introduced by Mr. Rogers, the humorist, whose efforts were greatly apprecia- ted. The hall and annexe were crowded to excess, and rarely has such a display of green " button- holes" been sported as was the case on this occa- sion. It may be here stated that nearly £5O were , realized last week for the War Fund, Infirmary and Dispensary, and the Indian Famine Fund, as the result of the Committee's decision to devote half of the takings at the door for the benefit of the funds named. On Monday evening Professor Delmar.the society entertainer and ventriloquist, was announced to appear, but owing to illness he was unable to do so. There was naturally a little disappointment but this was unavoidable. The management succeeded in engaging Mr. Mattie Cheshire for Tuesday even- ing who gave a capital entertainment. Mr. Nelson Jackson, the popular humorist and musical enter- tainer, appeared on Wednezday evening anti has given performances each evening since. Mr. Jack- son is an inimitable entertainer and has made him- self immensely popular with Warrington audiences. School children from People's College School and Heathside School were admitted at a nominal charge on Monday and Tues,'.ay afternoons. Though desirous of extending the privilege to an unlimited extent, it was found to interfere with the cookery lectures, and the committee felt. themselves obliged to discontinue the practice Tbere have been large audiences each day to wit- ness the famous tanaetican midgets, Piofessor Temple's cinematograph, and the other attractions , of the exhibition.

MILTON P.S.A., WIDNES.

At this meeting last Sunday there was a fairly good gathering, the Secretary occupying the chair. T Wailing sang two solos, "Consider the Lilies" and" Beyond the Clouds," in good style, Miss F Wailing being the accompanist. Mr A Harvey gave an excellent address on "Christians and Recrea- tion." He said that while there were several forms of recreation which might be indulged in and would be healthful under certain conditions, yet in many cases they were decidedly injurious. Foot- ball, billiards, and waist in themselves were good recreation, but if they were followed in such a way as to cause one to concentrate their whole thoughts and leisure upon them • they were undoubtedly harmful and ought to be avoided. WARRINIMON AND RUNCORN HARRIERS.

The Runcorn Harriers were visited on Saturday last by the Warrington Harriers, who of late have been tierformiag some remarkable feats. Warring- ton were without the services of Parsonage. while R Hadfield and J Cadman were absent from the Runcorn party. When the teams met at Warring- ton on February 3rd, the Warringtonians won by 32 points. Trail was laid over eight miles of good country in the direction of Rocksavage, through Sutton, returing home by Hall-wood, across the fields to Halton Brook, finishing up Halton-road. From the commencement of the race the Runcorn men were to the front, Hopkins, Ray, and Thomas being most conspicuous,Goodall gaining ground by his clever jumping. A more interesting finish could not have been witnessed, as the first five men were running a neck-and-neck race up to the crane,when Thomas drew to the front, and won the race. The position of the teams was as follows :—Runcorn : 1, Thomas; 2, Ray; 4, Goodall ; 5, Hopkins; 11, Shal- cross. Warrington: 2, Mather; 6, Price; 7, Mane- field; 8, llagnall; 9, Smith, a win for the Runcorn team by nine points. After the run, tea was pro- vided by Mrs. Richardson, at the Bull's Head, and a very pleasant evening was spent. MISS fIA3Re 'CONCERI PA.RTY AT 'RUNCORN.

RUNCORN BOATING CLUB

The committee of this progressive and enterpris- ing organization gave their members a much de- sired opportunity of passing a convivial evening together by means of a dinner and smoking concert held at the Wilson Hotel, on Thursday evening last. A large sad representative gathering of cm bites and friends sat down to an excellent repast at p.m., and after the tables were relieved of their tempting load, the evening's enter- tainment was proceeded with. The loyal toasts "The Queen,"Army Navy and Reserve Forces" were enthusiastically given. "The Visitors" was replied to very neatly by Mr. Coulton, supported by Or. Delany, and "The Boating Club," were responded to with gusto. The programme was excellent, items being contributed by Messrs. McDermott, T Hod- field (Liverpool) Walton (Northwich) T Johnson, Hopwood, and Grounds Mr. W Grounds acted as accompanist. The evening proved a great success and the object of the Committee which was to give members a chance of coming into social contact successfully attained. At the close of the proceedings the secretary, Mr. Aillinger proposed that the best wishes of the Club be exten- ded to Mr _Banks (a member) who had volunteered for the -Front, and who would probably sail in a few days. This was enthusiastically agreed to, and Vhe song " For he's a jolly good fellow " rendered heartily. Much praise is due to Mr and Mrs Sutton for the excellew; catering. A successful season in 1100 is confidently anticipated by the Committee.

A WIDNES EX-00111,s1CILL0R'S FAILIME. At the Liverpoll Court of Bankruptcy, last week Mr. Registrar Bellringer had before him for public examination a Widnes grocer, named Thomas. Carney, who carried on business in Wellington st, and Waterloo-road, and who for a number of years was a town councillor of Widnes. The sta.oment of alair, skowed liabilities £7ll,and assets:X:3B Mr. Poole, solicitor, Widnes. appeared for the bankrupt, In the course of examination by the Ofßcial Receiver the bankrupt stated that he did not think his public duties had interfered with his business, and that his failure was due to bad trade and bad debts. In 14 years he had booked over .Z 2,000 in bad debts, or an average of about .E 3 a week, he thought. Questioned further on this paint the bankrupt stated that he had been in the habit of trusting labourers and workpeople generally for necessaries such as be sold, and some of them in the end had contracted large amounts, of which he got nothing. The Official Receiver remarked that the bankrupt appeared for some years past to have been support- ing a good many of the families in Widnes. The bankrupt replied that this was so, because when he found that the father of a family was out of work he (the bankrupt) could not see them without food. Iso he gave them credit. He now saw that the re- sult of his phidanthropy was his own bankruptcy, but he considered that he would not have been in that position but tor his having been praised for money the last three or four months. The Official Receiver. I suppose the fact is you would not consider yourself insolvent so long as you could keep on getting credit? The Bankrupt.—Yes, get- ting credit with the intention of paying. The bank- rupt added that there was a good deal of the Wid- nes business done in the way he did his—it was a bad place for credit.—The examination was ordered ' o be closed.

RUNOORN URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL ELECTION.

Contest in Central Ward.

Very little interest has up to the present been taken in the election of Urban District Couociilors for Run- core, and five of the wards return candidates without an appeal to the polls. Central Ward, however, pro- vides some diversion and has to decide between Mr Joseph Hall Salkeld, and Mr Thomas Wood, both well- known gentlemen. At first it was rumoured in some quarters that Mr Salkeld was likPly to withdraw,but we now find that is not s: and that Mr Salkeld is going ta make a good stand. Up to the present.however, neither candidate has displayed much energy, but this might be due to the fact that the election day is yet some distance off. Though it looks as if the bulk of the ratepayers of the Central Ward, are as iadifferent and apathetic regarding their representation as were the ratepayers of the other Wards. Of the policies of the candidates it may be stated that Mr Satkoid gees in upon his own merits as an independent, whilst Mr Woodtinks or swims with the policy of the Runcorn Ratepayers' immolation. The returned members of the Council are :—Halton Ward, Mr Samuel Taylor; Heath Ward, Mr J W Light- burn; Newton Ward, Mr John Wilson ; Mersey Ward, Mr H G Grice ; Bridgewater Ward, Mr George Hewitt. All old members of the late Council. The vacancy seat in Central Ward was lately filled by Mr W Collier, who, however, does not srek re-election.

Warrington Town Council and Standing Orders. The following extract is from the "Councillor and Guardian ': —" An irregularity of an unusual char- acter gave rise to a somewhat warm discussion at the monthly meeting of the Warrington Town Council. A recommendation came up from the Paving Committee that tenders for granite eats from several quarry-owners in Lancashire and Cheshire shoeld be accepted. The matter would have gone through quietly enough .had not Mr. Greenin.; put an awkward question to the Vice- Chairman—Viz, had the tenders been advertised for in accordance with the standing orders of the Coun- cil. Mr. Heaton was compelled to admit that the standing order had not been complied with, and in defence of the committee's procedure said that, as there was a 'ring' or • corner' amongst quarry owners and merchants,they had endeavoured to de- feat the combination by gming outside. By doing so it was contended that the committee had effected a saving of from 25 to 30 per cent. The Mayor roundly condemned the action of the committee as subversive of good government, and furthermore asserted authoritatively that not a single local mer- chant belonged to the ring. An amendment that the recommendation be referred back was,however, defeated, and the committee's report adopted by fifteen votes to ten. For ourselves, we cannot but regard the action of the council in thus lightly condoning the committee's contempt for the stand ing orders as a grave error of judgment. Indeed we had far rather see a Council without such regu lations than that a committee should be allowed deliberately to ignore them. Apart from the ques- tion of principle involved, the committee's excuse is not altogether intelligible, for if there was any- thing calculated to defeat the tactics of a ring we should say it would be advertisement." RUNCORN AND THE PROPOSED TRANSPORTER BRIDGE.

Abandonment of Runcorn Ferry.

Attitude of the L. & N.W. Railway Company. A special meeting of the Runcorn Urban District Council, was held on Friday m)rning last, at the Town aall, for the purpose of considering and deal- ing with a Clause submitted by the PrOl/13ters of the Transporter Bridge,respecting the abandonment of Runcorn Ferry. There were present :—Messrs. Wiswall (chairman), E 'raison, W Handley, James Pritchard, Grorge Hewitt, S Taylor, J W Lightburn, T Jonas, It a Posnatt, HG Grice, D Bisbrown, F A Lake (law clerk), Marshall (secretary), and James Wilding (surveyor). Mr. W aandley, chairman of the Parliamentary, and General Purposes Committee, described the business before thent as purely formal. They were met for the purpase of passing the following resola-

tion "That the Council censent to the insertion of the pro-- posed clause for the protection of the Lendon and North Western Railway C 3., subject to the alterations mole therein, but that the promoters be informed that the Council would insist on tbe insertion of clauses requiring the minimum compulsory werking of the transporter at certain specified perio is, and providing for contract tictrts." The resolutioe was simply in continuation of the policy already adopted by the a:areal'. Since the meeting of the Gemmel es Mr Lake had cerrespended with Messrs. Aleop and Stevens, solicitors, Liverpool, and the following was a copy of a letter submitted to them wnich he (Mr. Flandley) thought they would all say faitefully stated the arrangements the cenernittee arrivel at the other day. No reply had been received up to the present : Copy :—Runeorn, 14th March, 190. Dear sirs,— Widnes and Runcorn Bridge.—l enclose copy of clause for protection of London and North-Western Railway Company, which my clients would approve as altered in red ink, but if such clause is inserted, and the ferry therc-by done away with my clients insist on some ciaase being aided, requiring a minimum compulsory working of the Transporter at certain specified periods and providing for not less than an hourly passage from midnizlit to 5 a.m. Also, that a clause or item be in- serted in the schedule of tolls providing for the weekly contract tickets for passengers at not more than lg. per week. Unless these points are granted, my Council, will have to reconsider their position both as to Ferry and Transporter.—Yours truly, Fred A. Lake (law clerk to the Runcorn Urban District Ceuncil), to Messrs. Aesop wed Stevens, Solicitors, Liverpool. Mr. Handley proceeding stated that the rights of the Ferry were vested in the two towne—Runcorn and Weines The promoters had forwarded the communication to them as they had no power to deelwith it themselves. The promoters were, however, perfectly willing to put such a clause into the Bill, if both Runcorn and Widnes were agreeable to it. As the clause was now presented to the Council he reasonably supposed it would he agree- able to them and alsa to Widneo Mr. Teike : Probably. Mr. Handley: Mr. Lake is in conernenication with Widnes, and it is very probable that they will pies a similar resolution. alr.Lightburn : Is it not as necessary for Widnes to pass a resolution in time f or the Committee of the House of Commons, as it is for us? Mr. Handley explained he had seen a telegram that stated the consideratioe of the Hill before the House of Commons had been put oN by mutual consent for a week, and there would be time enough before they next met for Widnee to pass a resolation. Mr Lake rather thought the VVidnes committee hal met the previous day Wednesday, and it Was very pro- bable they would submit the same resolution. He had not, however, heard the result. He could say, however, that he and the Clerk to the Widnes Council had con- ferred on the queetion, and were agreed upon the course they would advise their Councils. Mr. Handley went on to say that if the clause was inserted in the Bill, the op- position of the London and North !Western Railway Company, would be withdrawn, and that would be a wonderful help in enabling the promoters to get the hot passed. In fact they might almost say that with the removal of such oppeeitien as the Railway Company eeuid have presented, the bridge was almost, an assured fact. He did not think there would be much other opposition. The only one of any seriousness, he under- stood was with regard to the bridge being used for con- veying brine over to Lancashire. Mr Lightburn considered it a wonder they did not actually stop the tranpplanting of wood, and have it manufactured in Cheshire. Mr. Handley did not think much was to be feared at the present stage, from the brine question. The position with regard to it, was, the promoters of the bridge did not see their way clear to put in any negative clause, and as there was no power under the Act to sanction such a usage of the bridge, they simply wanted to leave it at that. The clause referred to in the resolution which Mr. Handley proposed was as follows : "For the protection of the London and North-Western Railway Company :—Whereas the bridge by this Act authorised is lot eeded to be constructed wholly or in part upon the site of an ancient forry,called the ltencorn Ferry, now the property of the London and North-Was. tore Railway Company (hereinafter called " The Ran- way Company")and power is conferree by this Act upon the Company to enter upon, take and use the highway or landing place attached to such ferry, upon the north side of the river Mersey, for the purpose of the new works. And whereas ureler the provisions of the London and North-Western Raitwey (lines near Liverpool) Act 1.861, the Railway Company have constructed and ever i since maintained n connection with the bridge carrying 1 their railway over .the river Mersey, immediately below the said ferry a bridge for the use of footpissengers. And svilereas the said ferry will be rendered useleas and superseded by the construction of the bridge by this Act, authorised, be it, therefore, enacted that from and after , the construction and opening for public traffic of the bridge and works by this Act authorised the Railway Company may and shall abandon and discontinue the maintenance and use of the said Runcorn Ferry." Mr J. Pritchard seconded, and remarked he thought it was understood some rime age, that supposing the L. and N.W. Railway Company were absolved from the cost of the ferry they would be disposed to con,ider some re-adjustment of the passenger fares for the Bridge. He did not know whether any condition sheuld be at- teched to a metter of teat sort, or whether, considering they were having a rival bridge across the Mersey they should leave the question to right itself. Mr. Lake observed the negotiations were not between the Council and the Railway Company at the present time, and he hardly thought it would be wise f et. any arrangement to be made between the competing com- panies. The tendency of the competition would be to reduce tolls, as a matter of course. Mr E unison did not like te move anything that would be at all likely to delay the matter. Having regard, however, to a new movement whica was going to make its appearance in the town, it would have b .en a very good thing if the Coancil had been able to act. ie accordance with the conditions laid down by the perhaps not an iaconsiderable section of ratepayers. The ques- tion under consideration appeared to he essentially one of those subjects where perhaps the advice of the greater number might save the lesser from making some mis. take. The tendency he alluded to seemed to be to set up not a rival chamber,but a second chamber, without the gilding. This would certainly lead to very great difficulty in the conduct of public business, because, if that principle was te he adopted, their attitude that morning ahou!d be something as follows :—lt was one of the principal matters, and it should perhaps be submit- ted to them for their judgment. There were many important things involved. There was the ques- tion of charges; and the question of hours of users, and the question of contracts. All these appeared to be essentially subjects for general discussion. But that morning they were anticipatieg the action of these ratepayers by the resolu- tion before them. Seeing that a number of gentlemen on that Council had accepted the conditions laid down, it seemed to him that they should take the first oppor- tunity of putting them into effect. Owing to the haste that appeared to bo essential in the matter, they were unable to do so. He should, however, like to hear an expression of opinion from these gentlemen, as to whether that was the sort of thing they should submit to anybody who appeared to be assuming to themselves to be a body of representatives. , He could only see as the result of that very series's diffi- culties in the future. Therefore at the very earliest stage he should like to take the opportunity of warning the Council against being subject to interference of that kind. Tne Chairman would no doubt rule he was wandering from the special subject for which the meet big had been called, therefore he would coine back to it. Ho did not entirely agree with Mr. Handley in his view that if the opposition of the London and No: th- Western Railway Company was withdrawn, it pra.cti- calle meant the collapse of all other opposition to the Bill. Rather, it appeared to him that opposition seemed to be gathering. They had a deputation from the Run- corn Rural District Council with them the other day,and se far as those gentlemen were able to pledg I them- selves they decided to go upon lino; similar to them- selves (the Runcorn Urban District Council). Since then that body had, however, decided to take no action in , support of the promoters of the Bill ; cone appearances seemed to indicate that unless satisfactory assurances I were given they might even oppose the B 11, especially 1 if the promoters :oold net see their way clear to insert a negative clause, for the prevention of carrying brine over to Lancashire. It did seem to he necessary that people should be prevented for all time from carryine brine from the county. Sa long As brine WAS esssntielly a constituent in the manufacture of chemicals, and so long as that beine was kept in the county; se long would they have chemical works carried on amongst them,and thus create a demand for labour. It would be seen there- fore, that any chemical workman in the too n and dis- trict Was interested in preventing time abstraction of brine from the county. He (Mr. Imisoa)rather admired

the way in which Mr. Dean had exercised himself, and stuck oat for concessions, with regard to the use of the Bridge at times when it would be a convenience to work- ingmen;and was pleased to testify to the way be insisted that the Bridge should be available for use at all hours during the night. Mr Doan also stipulated that weekly contract,: shou'd also be the subj-ct of future negotia- tions. Also he thought it should be said tuat in the event of the Promoters of the Bill not conceding the Councll'a terms, they had decided to prosecute their opposition to the Bill in the House of Lords.

Mr J W Lightburn had great pleasure in sup- porting the proposition. With regard to the first portion of Mr unison's remarks in regard to certain members being bound to coniult the ratepayers; inasmuch, as certain mernhers of the Conned had pledged themselves to submit all matters connected with the Council's business for their considera- tion before they toak action ; he (Mr. Liaison) was in error, in stating that they had done any such thing. Personally he had opposed it in as strong language as he claret What he had consented to—was what he would consent to then—that they were not conspirators against the public good, and that they were anxious so far as the interests of the ratepayers were concerned, to take the ratepayers into their confidence. The idea which had got abroad was certainly very incorrect. Mr. Grice also appeared desirous of saying something in further explanation, but the Chairman requested that the members refrain from saying anything further upon a subject that was out of order. The resolution was then put, and carried unanimously.

GILCHRIST LEG ruRE AT EARLESTOWN.

Sir Robert Ball on Invisible Stars.

Ths concluding, lecture of ttse series promoted by the Newton-in-Makerfield Lectures Comwitteei was given in the [own Hall, Esrlestawn, on Tuesday evening. The subject of the lecture was "Invisible Stars and how we see them," and the lecturer was Sir Robert Ball. Dr. Handyside presided, in the absence of Colonel Pil- kington M.P., and there was a full audience. The Chairman said they vviuld no doubt be as disap- pointed as he was that their member Colonel Pilkington was not abie to be present to take the chair. He had a letter from him stating that it would be impossible for him to attend, as a vary imp )rtant measure demanded his attendance at the Houss of Commons. He felt cer- tain that Sir Robert Ball needed no introduction from him. Their recept,ion of llias showed how well they re- membered his last visit to the hall. (Hear, hear.) ,Sir Robert Bail said his lecture was on "Invisible Stars," and before beginning, he might read a letter which he received lately just before de- livering the lecture at another place. It was anonymous, and he did not know where or -ho it was trim: —Dear sir—l see you are to lecture to-night- on what you call invisible stars. If the stars are invis ible, I shall be glad to know if you have ever seen them (Laughter.) Ought not a lecture on Invisible Stars to be about as entertaining as a concert of inaudible music ? Did you ever smell perfume from an inodorous flower ? Yours truly.—

Oa° who will be there.

Alluding to the shower of shooting stars which should have corns off last November, and which did not come off, he said he never had a better night's rest in his life, when many of them were anxiously looking for the shooting stars. A shooting star was a streak of bright light pascing across Lhe sky, but that streak of light was not a star in the ordinary sense of the word. It was qui.e a small thing even no larger than a grain of sand, but was moving tremendously quickly, a hundred times as quickly as the swiftest rifle bullet which was ever fired from a rile. If they picked up a bullet they would find it was quite hot, which was caused by boring through the air. So with the shooting star. It wen through open snece until it came in contact with oe air, and drilled a hole so to speak in the air Flying to the target it got warm, and it was heated 10,000 times as much as a rifle bullet, and forcing its way through the upper strata of the air, it got to white heat, and became a shooting star. At certain times of the veer these carne'in countless battalions, millions of them, and usually in November, and once every 33 years they came in a tremendous swarm. They all knew that the shower did not appear last year, but although it was not seen in this country there was a pretty general expectation that it would es seen in some parts of the world. There was a reasonable expectas ion that the shooting stars may te: seen next November, but if they did not come, they might nut by all thoughts ef seeing them for another 33 years. (Applause.) Thee should go out on a clear night, tie said, and raise their eyes to the sky. There they would see shining , above them some bright and beautiful gams, and many ' hundreds of small rears. Not get an opera glass to strengthen their view, and immediately the number of eters was increased ten fold. There they lay in profusion from the brightest glories of the firmament down to the feebleet pr;ents whose twinkles they could only jest discern hy glimpses. They should make a sup' erne effort to sound the depths of the Universe ' • the utmost extent of which their faculties were capable. They should call to their aid the experienced engineer, and the skilful optician, bid them construct the , mightiest instrument that their combined talents coald ' fabricate convey their telescope to the summit of a lofty mountain under the purest and most translucent sky which this globe had to offer, and thee, on the clearest and darkest night place your eye to that magic tube. There were displayed a host of sparkling points. innumerable as the sands on the sea shore, and so faint as to bs only just discernible when their vision was strained to the uttermost. Are we to Burmese, he asked, that even under these circumstances we have seen all the stars of heaven ? We have seen but the merest fraction of them. Out beyond, out far beyond that star dust which was the last thing our mightiest telescope disclosed, lay stare and systems of stars; which no human eye had ever seen or ever could see, even when every aid bad been invoked to strengthen the senses with which nature had provided us. These were the havieible stars. We were accustomed to the wonders of photography, and everybody kuew how the Rontgen Rays depicted objects which no human eye could see. It was the dry plates of the photographer which disclosed to us the invisible rays from the celestial objects. The plates that astronee mere used were so sensitive that were they employed in the ordinary practice of taking a portrait of a landscape the exposure for a hundredth part of a second would be anfficieet. But the invisible starlight was se faint that these earns plates had to be exposed to the sky, nct for mere s-couus or minutes, but often for eeveral hours. As the development of the plate proceeded, the bright visible stars first appear, then the telescopic stars break forth, and frequently the p'..ate was speckled with myriads of objects which were the pictures of stars that no human eye had ever beheld. Dere was a peculiar difficulty in photographing the stars a ith which every practical photographer would sympathise. The sitter would cot sit quiet. We could not arrest the diurnal MOVIII9O, of the heavens, but what we could do was to cause the whole apparatus to move smoothly and regu- larly so as to follow the stars. No machinery could be ri lied upon to carry the plate for ward with the precision Which was necessary for a perfect picture. Its perform- ance meet tos checked by the inc,ssant attention of the , observer who was seated at his post. By gently turning a handle to or fro he could so control the machinery that each star should be kept at the same point of the plate for hours until at last a perfect picture rewarded his patience. He showed on the sheet a picture of that remarkable reflector with which Dr. Isaac Roberts hod accomplished his magnificent work in depleting notable celestial objects. Such were the splendid weapene with which in these latter days astronon.ere had essayed once again to wrest their secrets from the heavens. It might be deliberately asserted that the instruments whose pictures had been placed before them, indicated as great an advance in the art of the Rssronomer, as the use of the telescope itself was an advance upon those primitive methods in which man had no other aid to search the celestial wonders than that which a pair of eyes aff srded him. His esteemed friend Dr. Gill, her Dalajesty's Astronomer at the Cape, had sent him for that lecture, a superb plate which he had taken of the most fernous star elueter in the heavens. In the centre lay a mars of stellar poiots so eloee together that they could hardly distinguish them separately, while all around were multitudes of the outlying stars belonging to the same system. Those stars were suns of etupendous magnitude. Suppesp that the opera- tions of the telegraph service were ram aed through the length and depth of space, Let one sire stretch from the earth to the moon, another from the earth to the son, another from the earth to the nearest brieht star. &nether from the earth to one of these ex- cessively remote invisible stars. Let them see the very shortest. time in which a message could be transmitted to each of these several destinations. The moon was so close to us that little more than a second would bare-

_ gun, d for a oignal to travel from the earth to our satellite. The sun was so far that eight minutes would have to elapse ere the electric waves, notwithstanding their velocity, which would place a girdle seven times round the earth in a second, would require not less than eight minute for the journey. Let them think of a telegraph wire from the earth to one of the invisible stars. It s?emed certain that the invisible star was so

remote that if the 4rlad tidings of the first Christmas at Bethlehem 1900 years ago had been disseminated

through the fsoiyerse by the swiftest messenger known to us,though th,t glad tidings sped its glorious way with a sin, el great enough to complete the circuit of the

earth seven times in each single secand,yet that invisible ssar was so excessively remote that all the seconds which had elapsed in 1899 years of the present era would not have sufficed for she journey. The sun and every star pour forth a multitude of beams of iron. Some of those beams belong to colours whits we can see, others consist of in visible lig ht; which could effect the photographic plate. Two photos of stellar spectra were shswn. A few years ago, he said, it would have been thought incredible that the feeble beams trom a star could have been made to disclose a panorama so full of detail and expressed so clearly as. that which the spectra exhibits. Great was the rejoic- ing among all who love to study the stars, when it was found that the spectroscope provided the means of dis- covering whether a star was coming towards t.ne earth or was retreating from it, and even for determiaing the pace at which the star's movement was urged. The im- portance of such measurements to the astronomer could hardly be overestimated. Not only did they give an element in the star's movement whicti must otherwise have remained utterly unknown but they enabled that movement to be discovered by a single observation in- stead of by a comparison of observations extending over an interval of centuries. Astronomers had long sought the explanation of the vagaries of Algol, the Demon Star, as we may well call it, the winking star. The problem lad however been solved with the help of the invisible light. Professor Vogel took a photograph of the invisible light, which showed that the Dimon Star was at the time hurrying away from us at the pace of about 26 miles a second. He desired to confirm his re- sults by a second observation, and found,that Algol was again moving at the pace of 24 miles a second. But judge of his astonis'ament when it ap- • peared that though on the former occasion he star was running away from him at this great speed, yet now it appeared to be coming to- wards him just as rapidly. A new light thus began to illuminate the mystery of the Demon star. It had always been known that the star winked, but the invisible light now added the further and most important circumstance, that before the wink the star was hurrying away from us, and after the wink it was hurrying towards us. If we pres“icl this to its consequences, the veil would he lifted, and the mystery ;of the winking star, inscrutable for ages, now at last stood revealed. Just as the heavens contained invisible stars, so they also contained invisible nabulal. Though the Pleiades have been gazed at for untold centuries, and studied by the mightiest instruments that have ever been designed to strengthen human vision, yet there was a glory eurrounding the group of stars that no human eye had ever seen. Modern research was gradually, making us in some degree acquainted with the marvel- lous oompl. xity and intertst of this thing which we call light. (Applause.) The Rev. J. Carson moved a vote .of thanks to Sir Rabert Ball. What they had heard to-night was the result of years and years hard and continual mental work, and therefore it should be more highly valuable, and they th inked him for the pleasure he had given them, and for the great instruction. (Applause.) Mr. C. W. Emmett seconded, and hoped that in the very near future the Gilchrist truitees would, grant them another course of lectures, and that from their list they would not omit the name of Sir Robert Ball. (Hear, hear.) Sir R Ball was mach obliged for the kind words which had been used by the proposer and seconder and to the audience for having passed the vote. Stoppage of Bold Hall Collieries. All the men who have been ernaged for the past four or five years in sinking pitshatts in the Bold Hall estate-at Clock Face, St. Helens, were paid off on Saturday, and the whole operetion of sinking and construction of shafts has been abandoned for the present. About 230 men have been discharged. It is believed that the company have stopped opera- tions in consequence of the high prices of fuel and iron. The two shafts which are intendel to be over 700 yards deep, have been only sunk to a depth of 180 yards. The stoppage will have a very serious effect en the little hamlet which has sprung up round the pit.

TWO YEARS OF LIFE IN GAYTHORN.

An Interesting Episode. The daily revelations .of our law onirts and facts in human experience furnish ample evidenle that occur- rences in rea, life are more wonderlul than any recorded in the imaginative productions of poet or novelist. This is true of life in all rinks of society and in every phase of existence. Among the working classes in Manchester there are experiences the relation of whicl may well excite our astonishment, as the foNw- ing narrative will show. An experienced iI chester reporter called upon Mrs. Kate Yexley, at No. 1, Chamba:s' Buildings, Jordan- street, Little Peter-street, Gaythorn, on Friday afternoon January 26,1900,and took from her own lips,in the pres- ence of her seconl daughter, the following story of the last two years of her life : "On the 29:h of April, 1898, I found it necessary to send for a doctor. I hal been suffering for some time from a terribly bad cough. Many times a day I got rid of large quantities of phlegm. A doctor came to see me from the Minches:er Royal Infirmary. He examined me and prescribed for me, and also told me to send for a trained nurse from the Nurses' Home, situated in The Crescent, Salford. I followed his instructions. The nurse came twice a day for three weeks or a month. She used to take my temperature every day. The doctor also coninoed to call. My cough got better, but there was no reduc'lon in the amount of the phlegm. Soon after this—about th- end of May—the doctor said I might go out a bit. When I told the nurse what the doctor had said, she observed that if he was not coming again to see me she could go also, and she ceased her visits.

' "Did the doctor come again ?" asked the reporter. " No," continued Mrs. Yexley, " but he told me I was to see him at the Infirmary as an (su:-patient." 'And did you go ?" " Well, let me tell you in my own way. It was on a Monday that I determined to walk out a bit, as the doctor had told me, and I went a short distance,but was glad to get home again ; 4ficl then I did a bit of sewing for my husband. It was while I was sewing that my right hand suddenly dropped to my side and became useless. The next day ( Tuesday) I started to g 9 to ,he Infirmary, but I could not get there. I had to come back. I staggered every step I took. I felt very bad. I then sent f n. the doctor:from the Infirmary,and he came to see me the next day (Wednesday). I told him I had lost the use of my right arm, He again examined me, sent me a bottle of medicine, also a" ruY.)bing bottle." _

"Something to rub on your arm ?" "Yes, and I used the application freely. Mc' nuiband and my daughter rubbed it on my arm right up to the shoulder, but to my surprise on the Friday Inorning the UM went out of my right leg also. WhPn my husband came home on the Sazurday afternoon—he had been away on the Friday— the neighb)urs told him how I was. I couldn't use my leg at all. It was like a wooden leg." " You were getting worse ?" pursued the interviewer* "I was very bad, I can as3uro you, and on Whit Sun- day my husband fetched another doctor, who pro- nounci-d me to be paralysed on the right side. He said I had he,nor.hage or some disease on the brain, and I must be removed at once. On Whit Monday I was un- conscious. Several doctors came to sea me. They or- dered me to be removed to the Infirmary, but there was no bed vacant until the Wednesday. I was taken to the Royal Infirmary on that day. When I had been there ah.:iut a week I heard the doctors and the young students talking r-iund my bed. They were talking about my case. I could not speak, although I could hear them." _

===== 0002601_19000323_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
"Did they operate upon you ?" asked the reporter.

"N', but I Was in the Infirmary seven weeks." " Did you regain the u3e ()your speech ?" was the next question. "Not until I had been there about eight or ten days. I had been unable to speak to anybody. After the seventh week they said I was to go to the Cheadle Convalescent Hoar!. I waq cluveved there and remained there eight weeks, claiefly in bed, but I was able now and then to go out into the G trdens, although for a long time I could not put my foot to the !„round. After being there two months I wanted to go home. troubled about my eldest daughter who is now 19 years of age. I could only walk with greatest difficulty. I still continued an out-p%tient of the Infirmary. I went there twice a week. I did that for abut two months. I could not use my hand, and I gave over going."

" How was that ?" " I got tired of waiting, and I used to be afraid lest I should fall while on my way." " Now tell me how it is you are as I see you to-day looking so well ?" " About 12 months ago last Christmas," resumed Mrs Ycx!ey, " I read in the • Manchester Evening News,' about a person residing in Dean-lane, Newton Heath, having been cured of paralysis by taking Dr. Williams' pink pills for pile people, and directly afterwards I de- termined to try a box. I took three boxes." "And when did you find any improvement in your Icondition ?" was the' next question. ' "Not until I was taking the second box. I then found that my power of speech was gradually coming back. I have hal two boxes since—five boxes alto- gether. I soon got to speak almost as well as Ido now. lam still taking the pills. I feel better now than I did [two years ago. I have got a good deal stouter. I am 40 years of age and I weigh 19 stone. When I went to Cheadle I weighed 16 stone, and when I came out caled 15 stone 'Jibs. I came out of the Cheadle Hospital on the 3rd of September, 1898." " You are certainly looking well now," observed the reporter," and when I called at your house leas evening I found you had gone to the theatre." " Yes," returned Mrs. Yealey,smiling, " I went with my daughter." " And now," added the reporter, "you have just been out in the pouring rain ?" "Yes, a person near here was killed suddenly by an acnident, and my second daughter and I have teen to attend the funeral." " Then you have every reason to speak well of Dr. Williams' pink pills ?" "I have, indeed. I have great faith in them. I have recommended them to other people, but Ido not know anybody who has taken them except those I have read about in the papers" "Had you any serious illness prior to two years ago?" "No, and I have been married 20 years on the 2nd of February next." "How old is your youngest daughter ?" "Eleven years." '1 I suppose you have no objection to your portrait being published, and your case being made public ?" " No, I shall only be pleased to let others know. I may tell you that before I went to Cheadle, and after I came back, I used to have pains in my back, which I am told are a usual accompaniment of paralysis, but they have gone. Dr. Williams' pink pills have entirely taken those pains away. Before I took the pills I used to have to lift my right hand to whatever place I wanted to put it with my left hand, but now I can lift it by itself (at the same moment raising her right hand to the back of her head.) Nobody ever thought, when I was taken to the Royal lafirmary, that I should ever come back alive. " You have told me you began to feel the gcyni effects of Dr. Williams' pink pills while taking the second bex ?" " Yes; after taking them I could feel a tingling in my right hand, as if the blood was circu'ating, and I began to walk bettne I can wslk along way now."

Mrs. Y.xley then showed a portrait .1 hirself taken 10 years ago. She looks as well now as then, but has grown much stouter.

Fatal Fall Downstairs.

At the Police Station on Saturday afternoon, Mr S Brighouse, held an inquest relative to the death of Ellen Fog, 71 years of age of 63, Ellesmere- street. Evidence was given to the effect that the deceased had been suffering from influenza for about a month and was miler the care of Dr Smith. On Sunday, March 11th, she was not well and went upstairs to bed, but when half way up she fell to the bottom. Dr. Smith had sent a note to the Coroner to the effect that the fall had killed the deceased. She died on Friday morning last.—The jury returned a verdict of si lieeldoital death."

NORTHERN UNION CUP COMPETITION. Fine Performance by Warrington.

St. He!ens Defeated at Knowsley-road.

Warrington, in the first round of the Cup Competition, had one of the hardest tasks in the draw set before them, as they were obliged to tackle Sr. Helens on their own ground: The Saints have done very well in the League Competition and as they defeated Warriagton at Wilders- pool, there was plenty of confidence in the glass town that they could do it again. Warrington,however, by no means feared the encounter and went forth determined to enter the second round somehow. Their recent smart displays encouraged them, and at the same time en- couraged their supporters, who to the number of a thousand or two travelled to St. Helens. The crowd there was not very large, and the Warrington contin- gent seemed to be the stroagest. Though snow was ' falling heavily when the special trains laft Warrington everything was satisfactory at St. Helens, the ground being in perfect order. Both teams turned out as selected, St. Helens having to take the field minus Foulkes at full back. Mr. Prfestley (of Broughton) was the referee, and the teams lined out as fellows : Warrington : Hallam, full back ; Fish, Isherwood, Duckwortu, and Hockenhall, threequarters ; Bate and Burgess, half-backe ; Taylor, Morrison, Mereweather, Boardman, Scholtze, Elea, Swift, and Antriebus, for- wards. S. Helens : Mercer, full back ; Liversage, Barnes,' Traynor and Doherty, three-quarters ; Boyle and Apple- ton, half-backs ; Briers, Green, Bentley, Thorns m, Dale, Mooney, Prescott,and Whiteley.forwards. Promptly to time Taylor set the bail in motion bef ere 3,000 spectators, and against the breeze. The ball went m touch, and was returned by Traynor, Boardman making a mark which settled play in the home quarter. St. Helens got possessien, but Traynor was slow and Isherwood intercepted, the ball being sent to the line where Hoekennall made a goad attempt to score. A feul tackle by Traynor was penalised, and Fieh had an abor- tive shot at goal. Warrington kept on the offensive, Bate, Isherwood and Fish putting in a pretty round or passing which Mercer only checked in time to oblige Fish to kirk dead. St. rielens even- tually got into the home half through the agency of Dale, wholfollowed up finely. The home team's efforts were very crude, and the smart tackling of the War- rington men soon averted any danger. Liversage put in one or two good runs down the touch line, but was always bowled in touch on coming to Hockenhall. The game was kept going at a fast rate and was of the typical cup-tie order, shirt rushes and prompt tackling being the general order of things. Fish had another penalty kick, but was a long way off the mark, and then the Saints rushed the oval to the Warrington line where Hallam saved splen- didly. For a time the home team showed some smart- ness, Barnes putting in a spanking run into the War- rington quarter. Appleton got clear from the scrum- mage and fed Liversage, but the wing man was too well watched to get far. Faulty play by Traynor enabled Warrington to clear, and the game was soon being contested in the home quarter. Mereweather headed a grand dribble which looked certain to result in a try when Mercer brot•ght off a daring save. Beck came the Saints with a rush, Fish only just saving his die. A poor kick on his part sent the ball to Deherty, and the wing man had only to pick up to score. Instead he knocked on. Warrington got the upper hand again afrer this, Fish running grandly to the St. Helens' quarter flag. Liversage had a warm haniful given him, and mulling the catch owing to Duckworth's 6050 attention, he presented Hocketahall with an easy try. No goal fol- lowel, but the chenille was tremeneous from the War- rineton section. The reverse roused Sc.Helens and they made several spasmodic efforts to score, but tne men in front were too smart for them. Half-time came with Warrington a. try ahead. Traynir restarted,and Warrington were soon attack7ner with the aid of the breeze. Mercer got ternus,anel The Warrington backs played on him continuously, with the result that Se Helens were almost centinually defending. Burgess tried hard to squerzi over, but was held up on the line. Sr. Helens had a very anxious time here, but et last Bate was penalised, and the kick brought them relief. Both half backs were as fault in putting th- ball in, and a number of free kicks against Warringten gave St. Helens a chance. Hallam was repeatedly 'ailed upon, but acqaitted him- self well, and the Beige was raised eventually. Mer- cer let his side down again and Warrington got near the line. Bate secured and punted across for Fish. The ball hit the posts and Fish being near caught it on the bounce and scored smartly. He aeain failed at goal from the easiest of places. St. Helens were now quite over-played, and though they occasionally entered the Warrington half they never looked like scoring. Five minutes from the finish Mereweether was injured and had a fin, Warrington being left with fourteen men. Hallam was cheered for a lengthy punt and tackle of Mer- cer, St. Helens again having to act on the defensive. The final soon came, Warrington winning with ease by two tries (6 points) to nil.

Runcorn V. Birstall.

The want ef a qualifying round in the Northern Union Cup Competition was amply illustrated through- out the country last week: Perhaps as pitiable an example as any, was the meeting at Runcorn between the above teams. Runcorn gave several of their princi- pal players a rest, but the team that did fee!! Birstall possessed such an overwhelming superiority that it were absurd to attempt recording the game in detail. In the words of the crowd it was a " tea party" Runcorn were having, and lucky for Birstall's self conceit that the Linnets did treat the matter as a joke ; else, pnchance, Birstall would never raise up their heads again. Tries were scored by Jones (4), Faulkner (3), J Butterworth (2), Lightfoot (2) and Woods. Farmer, however, out of 12 attempts only succeeded in improving three, which was a pity seeing what a grand opportunity for practice he was throwing away. Final result :—Runcorn, 3 goals, 12 tries (42 points) ; Birstall, nil.

The Indian Famine

Atfurgent appeal is being issued by the Warrington Indian Famine Relief Committee to the inhabitants of the town and district appealing for funds on be- half of famine stricken India. The appeal has been sent to the various works and the employere are asked to open a subscription list at their works The appeal states that our great Eastern Depen. dency is again confronted with a famine of unpre- cedented severity, and the pathos and tragedy of the situation are emphasised by tne plague at Bom- bay. Extensive districts. with a population of 60,000,000, are more or less affected ; and in some parts the famine is so severe that 90 per cent, of the cattle are lost, and the distress is spread- ing rapidly. Already nearly 4,250,000 people— a number exceeding the population of Lanca- shire— ara being saved from actual starvation by Government relief, and many millions more are on the verge of destitution. During the famine in 1897 the people of the United Kingdom remitted to India £773,000 and of this amount Lancashire con- tributed the magnificent sum of nearly £160,000. It is impossible to estimate the value of the timely and generous help that was extended to India in the time of her extremity three years ago. The fearful suffering can scarcely be estimated which would result if, in consequence of our present troubles and anxieties, the same generous assistance were not rendered now. It must be re- membered, too, that to alleviate our troubles and anxieties India, in common with our Colonies, has extended a helping hand to us. When the last Indian Famine Fund was raised Lancashire was passing through a time of great commercial depres- sion. Now the position is one of general prosper- ty and although the benevolence of the public has been severely taxed, another generous. reqsouse will surely be made to the cry for help, from the suffering millions of India Everyone can lend a helping hand, is will maintain a poor widow for a week. 203 will maintain a family for f a month ; £lOO will supply the seed- grain and plough cettle to enable a whole village to make a fresh start.—The local committee has also decided to make a street collection at an early date.

War; or Pusillanimous Peace.

Sir.—May I say to" Iona" that I have far too much respect for his earnest convictions—and those like- minded with him—to be " wroth" with hitn or them. But at the same time I (I, not aduaic their monopoly in earnestness of conviction. And I think ir of the highest importance that Brit4ns should be unanimous on an occasion so grave as this. We should be fully prepared te resist aggression ; and as fully prepared to withstand the temptation to be aggres- sive. I hold with " lona " that no Christian nation could conscientiously go to war. The Scriptural aphorisms " Resist not evil" and" Turn the other cheek" would alone prPclude so glaring a contravention of the vr,rds of the mild Galilean were any of the nations truly Christian. But in this sense I feel like the Bishop who heard the sailors in the fo'castle swearing during the storm. I can thank God for it ! The sooner " lona " and his co-thinkers recognise the fact that in this as in other matters we are a pack of humbugs, so much sooner will they have their eyes open to the actual facts of existence. Our Christianity is only adap- ted for Sunday wear and parade purposes ;it will not stand the rough test of practical every day work; it won't wash. Mr. Kruger is often quoting Old Testament texts. How pleased he would nave been had we con- fined ourselves to quoting, and acting on, New Testa- ment texts solely ! Britain would quickly meet with the fate it deserved did this 'settle-Alt propaganda prevail.— I am, sir, yours Ste., • Penketh, March, 1900.

JOHN PEERS.

===== 0002601_19000323_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN NOTES.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT.] The special meeting of the District Council, last Friday, seems to indicate pretty clearly that in the minds of the Runcorn Urban District Coancillors— or at any rate the majority of them—there appears to be a conviction that toe body to fear as oppo- nents of the Transporter Bridge are the London and North-Western Railway Company, and by reason of this conviction the Council run the risk of being put into a false position. They must decide whether the proposed Transporter Bridge is going to be a boon to Runcorn; they must see, if it is going to be erected at ail, that the working men of Runcorn are provided for; and that a fair and equitable tariff is fixed with acceptable conditions as to time of using, etc. Being satisfied upon these questions, it is the duty of the Urban Coancil to support the Bill. There is no necessity for passing clauses for the protection of any private company; and, whether the London and North-Western Railway Company oppose the Bill or not,—thy certainly will not support it,—should matter not at all to Runcorn's city fathers.

Again! What effect does the clause bring about? Nothing more or less than the abandonment of the Runcorn Ferry! The abandonment, says onie- one, of what has been "a dead letter" for a con- siderable time. But, I wish to point out, for special consideration, at a time when everybody seems in- clined to commit themselves, that the Ferry tills a very diplomatic position ; and, while it remains, it acts as a check upon the London and North-Wes- tern Viaduct and footway, as well as upon any other Bridge or device Lir spanning the river that may be in future time facilitating the traffil be- tween the two counties—Cheshire and Lancashire. The decision the Council have came to is in my opinion a wrong one. The Runcorn Ferry should be kept open for all time; or at least until such time as there exists a bridge wholly controlled by the public trusts. If, however, the London and North-Western Railway Company consider they are suffering an injustice, in being compelled to keep open and work the ferry, their remedy is to see that the promoters of any new undertaking parti- cipate in that burden. Still the Company cannot be blamed for an attempt to get rid of a burden,and no doubtj are congratulating themselves upon a politic stroke.

A 3 the same case applies t) Widnes, and as I am given te understand trot the Corporation of Widnes have not yet decided upon the matter, I express the sincere hope they will not be so blind as the Run- c .irn Council in snapping eagerly at the bait. Neither town has much to thank the L. and N.W., Railway Company for, and there is no reason why _

they should -so hastily and hurriedly express a willingness to confer favours upon thoqe whom we have paid so dearly for the little benefit derived.

The Runcorn Ratepayers' Association held a meet ing on Monday evening, which was very sparsely attended. Messrs. John Wilson and H G. Grine (members o the District Council) were there, how- ever, and delivered addresses. Mr. John Wilson gave the Association some fatherly advice, and practically bade them "organise themselves getting into touch with the whole of the Rate- payers." He was in sympathy with them, but how much better would it not b 3 to know that that gathering was backed by the bulk of the Ratepayers of Runcorn. This is just what the Association should take to heart, it they would succeed, for the question: Whom do they represent? is being asked in more than one quarter.

The policy of the Ratepiyers' Association is now said to be :—" (a) To protect the interests of the ratepayers by a strict supervision over all local expenditure of public moneys; (b) to record votes of representatives on all questions directly affect- ing the ratepayers' interests; (c) to promote the return of representatlve3 (unbiassed by any religi- ous or political motives) to the governing bodies of the town ; (d) to protect purity of voting at elec- tions for district councillors generally ; and to further any other purpose conducive, in the opinion of the committee to the interests of the town."

A decidedly vague policy, must be the opinion of all who read; and, as in its vagueness, it is identical with the policy of the present District Council.how xnuch better off are the townspeople going to be from a furtherance of it? No! My dear Associa- tion ! "Interests of Ratepayers generally, etc., etc.," is far too vague, and is a policy every member of the Council present and past have at one time and another issued as part and parcel of their election address.. You must really give us a definite policy, on questions of the hour. if we are to embrace you openly. Get to work! as Mr. Wilson suggests and as I now supplement. Get to work bringing your bast forces .to bear; and, having decided, some definite policies on such questions of: purchasing the Gas Works ; installation of electric lig!lt ; park and recreation ground ; reduction in dock and rail.; way dues; street improvements; transporter bridge, ferry or no ferry ; but, if you are of opinion—as xpreesed by some of your promoters—that the own requires all these, but cannot afford them throw up the:sponge and let things go on as they are.

FOOTBALL.

About last Saturday's match I wisb to say nothing It could not be taken seriously. Of next Saturday's event, too, I wish to reserve my judg- ment. The fixture is Runcorn v. Maryport, at Run- corn, and contains possibilities too awful to con- template, What if Runcorn decide to play those Unix. who were resting last week? I should rather suggest that the whole fifteen be allowed to rest, so that Maryport may have a—but there I wish to reserve my judgment.

Death of, the Rev. P. J. Byrne. Many of our readers will hear with regret of the death of the Rev P J Byrne, who was for two years curate of St. Arms Church, Warrington, which took place at the residence of his brother•in-law, Dids— bury on Sunday afternoon. Smne months ago the rev, gentleman was seriously indis- posed and when he had somewhat recovered he was advised by his medical attendant to visit Bournemouth. He did so but derived no benefit. The cause of death was an internal complaint, The deceased gentlemen, during the time he was in Warrington, WRA very popular and was a most energetic worker. He was a friend of tne Temper- ance cause and on one occasion app.mred at the Police Court in opposition to a licensing applica- tion. On leaving the town for the curacy of St. Peter's Birkddle, he was the recipient of a handsome present from the congregation of St Anns.

A SHARP SKIRIVIISH.

Heavy Losses Among the Boers._

LOBATSI, March 16. w,

The enemy is advancing in force from Hafeking towards Lobatsi. Lieutenant Tyler, of the West Riding Regiment, serving with Colonel Plumer,was killed yesterday by a shell.

LOBATSI, March 15.

Firing has been proceeding a few miles to the south of Lobatsi'this morning. The enemy's 11b. Maxim was freely used. Colonel Bodle came in touch with the enemy this morning just in time to prevent his camp from being surprised. The Boers tackled our advance party,and captured a few boxes of ammunition, and nearly secured a Maxim. Lieutenant Chapman's horse fell in a hole. and Lieutenant Chapman was captured. Corporal Galt hal a hand-to-hand scuflla with the enemy. He lost his gun and his bandolier, but Colonel Bodle coming up pat the Boers to flight, inflicting heavy loss on them, and Galt escaped. The enemy are now within four railes of Lobatsi. LOBA TSI, MARCH 16.

Yesterday afternoon the enepl,y pressed closely on Colonel Plumer's main camp and kept up a hot fire from a one-pound Maxim, killing one white and one native. They have now placed a one-pound Maxim and a 121-pounder on a hill on the east side of the line 4,000 yard s to the south of the main camp. Our right fia nk is protected by the chief Bathoen, who has warned the Boers not to enter his territory. It is improbable that they will do so. Since their ill-advisel raids oa Linchwe the Dutch have VnOIBROIn9 dread of the natives.

The burv,heri are reported to be leaving the environs of Aaf,Jking, leavirlg only sufficiAnt artil- lerists to man their big guns there. No Boers are at Ramathlabama, where the beseigers of Mafeking were supposed to have their base. Colonel Plumer's scouts visited. Etamatillabs,ma yesterday. The usual Mafeking news budget is overdue, but no anxiety is felt on that score.

Dynamite explosions are heard at intervals, indi- cating that the enemy are wrecking the railway to the south of Lobatsi.

Another Attack on Mafeking. The Boers Repulsed

CAPE TOWN, TUESDAY Mom NING.

A despatch received here this morning states that the Boers have made another attack upon Mafe- king, but were repulsed. They, however, recom- menced firing heavily upon the town. NATAL.

Pomeroy Burned by the Boers.

From Natal come tidings of the destruction by the Boers of the town of Pomeroy, near the Big garsberg. Bethune' s Mounted Infantry arrived too late to save the town from being fired. The Boers, 5,000 strong, are reporteii in a strong position on a hill above the ruins of Pomeroy.

More Runcorn Volunteers for the Front.

Quite an appregiative audience assembled at the Dril Hall, Runcorn, last S t turday night, when the series of concerts, given by the Runclrn V Auntcrrs, in aid of

the Loctl War fund, was brought to a close with an effort that proved a fitting climax. 'ihe principal artistes were : Miss Eunice Bailey (St. Helens), Miss Phoebe Elumpbreys and Miss Barbara Johnson(Runcorn), Mr. Bantoek Pie.rpoint and Mr Harry Bamber(Liverpool) The Runcorn Volunteer B and also contributed two items, and to the Band belonged the honour of opening both parts. Their first item was the march "V;ctorious,"ane their second a descriptive fantasia "The Relief of Lady- smith." Mr. Harry Bamber was responsible for "Lily of Killarney"and "She is far from the Land,"and accounted for them meritoriously. Bing recalled for the latter song he gave "The Commissionaire." Miss Phoebe Humphreys, a young contralto of exceeding promise effectively contributed "Swallows" and "Kathleen Mavourneen." Mies Eunice Bailey of St. Helens already a great favourite with the Runcorn public, enhanced her reputaton with the song "For all Eternity " and " Leave me not" and as recalls she gave " Killarney " and another. Miss Barbara Johnson executed the rOanoforte solo " Erl King," with appreciative expression, and was heartily applauded. She also filled the post of a very efficient accompanist. The success of the evening,however, was the popular baritone, Mr Bintock Pierpeint, who fully sustained his highest reputation. That his efforts were duly app:eciated may be gathered from the fact that though down for but three songs the delighted audience would not let him off under seven. They included 66 Etenoar and Arms," 66 Father O'Fiynn," " the Absent- minded beggar," " Obedient to the Call,"" Soldiers of the Queen,"" The man who carries the gun" and "Old Ireland's Sons are bound for death or glory." A co!lec tion followed the " Absent-mindel Beggar." During the interval, Captain George F Ashton (commanding to e Runcorn Detachments, E. Ches. Vol. Batc.) said as that was the last concert the Vollanteeri would give, it was perhaps his duty to say that the Volunteers of Run- corn had been able to get together by other methods and those entertainments about £lOO for the local war funds. (Applause.) They would remember a short time ago Oat five members. of the Runcorn Company had left for the front. (Applause.) Well, he wished to say that a further five had been selected as a waiting company their names were : Corporal William Parkin- son, Lance-Corporal Williim Griffiths, Private w Charlesworth, Private W Clarke, and Lance-Corporal J Edgecock. (Anplause.) With regard to the statement in the papers that some 70 or 80 Runcorn V.)lunteers bad given in their names for active snelice, he supposed the reason nothing further had been heard about that offer was because of the successes which were attending British arms in South Africa at present—(loud applause) —and therefore they would not be required. (Con- tinued applause.) Still it was pleasing to know they were ready te go. He concluded by moving a hearty vote of thanks to the artistes and all who assisted in the sucless of the several entertainments. Trio proceed hap concluded with the singing of the National Anthem Arrival of the Warrington Volunteers at Capetown,

The Warrington Voluntee-s 63 in number, under the command of Captain F M Appleton, arrived safely at Capetown on Saturday, on the transport, Avondale CASCIO, which has altogether over 1,000 volunteers on board, namely, Volunteer Companies Royal Lancaster, lit and 2ad Northumberland Fusiliers, Liverpool, West Yorkshire, South Lancashire, Derbyshire, Yorkshire Light Infantry, and Durham Light lafantry, or a total of 1,048.
