THE SOUTH LANCASHIR:i REGIMENT'S NEW

COLONEL.

Lieutenant Colonel R. H. Hall,

As duly announced in the " Examiner " of a fortnight ago, Major R II Hall has been appointed lieutenant-colonel of the South Lancashire Regiment, vice the late Lieutenant Colonel McCarthy O'Leary, killed in action in Natal. His appointment has just been gazetted. Colonel Reginald Hawkins Hall was born in 1854, and was first connected with the South Lancashire Regiment in 1873, when he was appointed lieutenant from the Militia. From 1883, when he received his captaincy, to 1885 he was adjutant nf the regiment. He was promoted major in 1893. Since 1898 he had been second in command. The new colonel, who possesses certificates for musketry (extra), signalling, eac., specially distinguished himself in the study of military law. Though the, present campaign is his first experience of active service, he has been a good deal abroad, having been in South Africa (1884 to 1887), at the Straits Settlement. Gibraltar, &e. He was for some time adju- tant of the let Vol. Batt. South Lancashire li,tgirnent, at Warrington. Colonel Hall is one of the biggest men in the regiment, standing 61t. 24in.

(1) by slaughtering a pig without the permission of the military authorities.ard (2) with having failed to report the death of the said pig to the secretary of the Supply Committee. The accused made a statement to the effect that they fell in with the pig while on ()try on the veldt, killed it and brought the carcase into camp. Cheshire Imperial Yeomanry. Letter from Captain Mosley Leigh.

!I On Saturday morning, Mr J Arthur Cowley, clerk to the Northwich Utban Council, read a cotninunicarion from Caprain Mosley Leigh, headed 2nd Battalion 22nd Company Imperial Yeomanry, 22nd February, in which he says :—" lam afraid I cannot give you very much more news than what is in my last letter, as one day on board ship is very much like another. We are all long- ing to get on land, and we hope to get to Capetown to- morrow, Saturday, afternoon. We have amused our- selves with cricket, boxingsand concerts. The N ntbwich Company held their own. They won the cricket tournament. Rigby and E Barnes bowling7well, and taking all the wickets. We also won the light weight boxing. Lambourne, from Nantwich, being our cham- pion. Lancashire won the heavy weight. We have supplied plenty of musical talent. All the men are very well, but wore pulled down by inoculation, They are, however, much bAtter for their voyage, and they have imnroved wonderfully in drill, shooting, and general app.aranv, Their conduct has been excellent. We, have lost a lot of horses, all nearly dying from pneu- monia. Oyer 40 he, 5'3 altogether have gone overboard. My company lost ter. lam longing to get to work with my men all mounted, and then soon hope to have them ready for the front. The Elder Dempster people have done their very teit to make us comfortable, having given the men plsnty of t ,bacco, every kind of games you can imagine, besides a writing case and paper tor each one on blard. Remember nie to all good friends at North wich."

"A Painful Thing to get Through."

The following letter is from the pen of Mr Tom Baxter (a s m of Mr Thomas Baxter, Dutton Hall,) who has gene to the frent with the Cheshire Imperial Yeomanry (Cap- tain Leigh's Company). It is addressed to the writer's Transport Lake Erie, February 23rd, 1900. D:Ar writing this as we believe on the ore of cur arrival in Cape Town. We shall not be sorry to tread sail once more, although I got qaito used to sailing, and have not felt the !east bit sea-sick. We've seen and been passed by several ships. This morning we meta deal horse floating by. se I guessed we were foPerving in the wake of another troop-ship. Oar germ'so was right. We are now broad side on her, she looks about 100 gars off, but is probably a mile; think she is broken down. We hive bad several break- downs, tut never had both propellers stopped at once. We are quits hardened to seeing horses die and thrown overboard. We have lost about 45 out of 300 horses and shall lose several more. We sleep under the horses, and it is vary nice to have a sick horse doing a war dance over you all night and all the stokers just under you. I have been both inoculated and vaccinated (nothing now hut to be cremated), so have taken all precautions in that way. The formlr is a very painful thing to get through. They placed us all on the mess deck fleor after the operation, which is s'ight, but for about 48 hours we are Trite useless. They inoculate in the side. We got frigh-fuly sore with lying on the fl )or with only a couple of blanketi under us, and we had to lie on one side or on the back. Half the Eaton troop were done first in a hatch, but the ethers seeing what the first lot were going through when asked who would be done, six only accepted the invitation. Thirty six of our company were done(mestly my acquaintances)one of the Warwick- shires, and not a single Lancashire. Captain Leigh was indoors about a week. It came fearfully het all at once. blistered our arms and faces and fairly made us 'sweat,' but has been cooler since we crossed the line. We are very well fed in a plain way. We are twelve at a table. At ours there are Cooke, Stubs, Rigby. Busby, Baird, Thompson, Dr. Felton (a private). Smethurst, Bowers, Scwale, and Lord the jockey. We do -mess orderly in turns; two each day, and have to draw forage, set tables wash up, and wash floors. For breakfast we get porridge first, salt herrings second, and last, and worst of all, Irish stew. For dinner we get rough j nints of beef or mutton with prt atoes, and bully beef, and kippers fer tea, which are very good. There is always an ample supply of sp'endid bread and butter and marmalade, which we nearly live on. . On Sunday we get plum puelclines. There is no limit to the supply of everything. The hospital has been full all the journey. I am sorry Heatley and Hoplev, the elder are amone the sic.k. I have been very well. The Elde De.mpiter Co., gave every man a writing case and pape pencil, let., and a lb of tobacco. Have got !lb now

Don't know when I'll smoke it. A match is worth ani ounce of 'bacca as we can't buy them on board. We have had a fair amount of drill on board, but not much shooting. Tney can only throw a barrel out; and peg at I it. We have bad several concerts, and cricket matches. the 22 ,d Company winning the final. Tony put netting up and have to knock the ball over different lines for runs. A boxing contest, the officors giving a medal and £2. for over and ander eleven stone. Then we have bathing on deck. The tffieers practised shooting at sharks. There are several fellows had punishment for refusing to obey, &I. You get a week as orderly, carry your kit three hours a day about deck, and other things. One fellow gct the kit for ten daFs and is now under loch and trey 80m3where in the hold with a guard. I intend to keep a clean reaord if possible. Of coufne there are some completely bor-e.,- sick and would do anything to get back. The troops are in the best of spirits. We never think about war; I expect it is with hearing nothing of it. _ _

"Saturday evening. seven o'clock. 24th. 800-ray! We are in Taole Bay. We sighted land about two o'clock You should have heard the cheer. A gunboat has just told us through a speaking trumpet that Kimberley it relieved and Ladysmith is expected to be to day. We have anchored for the night and shall disembark to morrow. We have been busy dying belts darker in strong coffes. The officers have been firing at sea-gulls this afterno 7n and killrd several. I caught sight of our prisoner to-day in a cell. You would laugh if you saw us. We have all te?n clipped with machines and lo.)k like a load of convicts.

"Sunday evening. So near and yet. so far. We have been anchored all dey about a mile out and are rill,but are under ceders to be ready by ten in the morning. I believe the other Cheshires are going first to Maitland, about .four mi!es off Capetown, to get the tente while wa follow with the horses. Several tugs came this afternoon and sold us pap-rs. We gave a shilling each. Some efficers and doctors came toe. We had a full dress parade. There are some scores of transports anchored here, oae with 1,100 men on not unloaded yet. Cape- town seems a very pretty place from the sea. They say we are only for a week's training and then off to the front. I bop e so, as I am very ameons to see some o the fighting, as are most of the others- " Tura lay morning, Maitland Camp. Landed at last. We came up yesterday. I had an Experience of trans- port. Rode up as escort on a waggon with the kits. We were four hours getting four miles. T;le camp is like a big waste common, as some thousands of troops are camped here. I was working hard pettiag tents up all day, %nd then was on guard all night. We had to hold the horses, as it got dark before we c-elld tie them up We had nothing to eat yesterday, except at break- fast, bnt think things will work right now. We are twelve in a tent, have only a blanket for a bed and clothes too, with our kit for a pillow. About 200 j Besuto ponies were driven in yesterday and put in kraal. I expect thy are for some of us. All the work;. people here are nigeers. I will close this now and try and get it posted. D )n't think we shall be kept here lone—Your affectionate brother, Tow"

MILITARY NOTES.

Captain Plomer, Ist battalion Liverpool Regiment serving at Warrington wit o the depot companies has bien appointed irljutant of the nmw 3rd bat- talion Kings Liverpool Regimmt, now being formed.

Major Hall was on Saturday promoted Lieutenant Colonel, to command the Ist battalion South LAnca- shire Regiment. in succession to the late Lieutenant Colonel McCarthy Olean,. Captain Hume of the 3rd Battalion South Lanca- shire Regiment with headvarters at Warrington has been given a commission in the regular service becoming second-lieutenant in the Ist Battalion South Lancashire Regiment. Re joined the 3rd Battalion South Lancashire Regiment in 1897 and was promoted Captain in the corps last July. Captains Nicholson and Swann have been pro- moted to the rank of Major in the Liverpool Regi- ment on the increase of the number of field officers in the corps as the result of the formation of the new 3rd and 41 battalions of the regiment. In addition Lieutenants Parnieter, Bailey, Leader, Harington, Armitage. Brush, Sheppar 1 and Pin- well have been promoted to the rank of Captain in the Liverpool Regiment. Captain Bailey has of late been serving with the Depet Companies at . War- rington _

Captain Jobling of the 6th battalion Liverpool Regiment at Warrington has gone to South Africa and has been attached to te Ist battalion Durham Light Infantry for duty as a subaltern.

It has bPen'Adel that the 6th battalion Kings Liverpool Regiment shall be embodied at Warring- ton at the commencement of May. The corps will probably train at Salisbury plain. At the same time the 3rd battalion Cheshire Regiment will assemble

at Chester and the 3rd and 44 battalions Manches- ter Regiment at An-under-Lyne. All the Lan- cashire and Cheshire militia battalions will then be doing permanent dut y.

The Seige of Kimberley. A Warrington Man's Experiences Interesting Letter.

Mrs Brookes, 13, Hoyle-street, Warrington, has this week received an interesting letter from ner son Private S. Brookes, of the Loyal North Lancashire Regiment which formed part of the garrision at Kimberley during the seige. It is deted February, 1900, and reads as follows.--" I write these few lines in answer to your letters which are dated 18th October, and 3rd of Decem- ber and which I only received on the 21st of this month along with all the papers that you have sent since then. I also received the pArcel which you sent me and I can tell you that the cakes that were in it went down all right after living on the rations we have been on this last two months and to mend matters we had just landed in after being out on the veldt for 48 hours. I wish to say thet we were relieved here on the 18h of February and I can tell you that it was a surprise to us when we heard that there were troops so near to us. When we heard of them being so near to us we were sent out to try and drag the Boers out to " serap "with us but they had got to know as well as us that there were troops at hand and they had made off the shortest way. Of course we walked straight into their entrench- ments without firing a shot. We slept in their entrench- ments that night and sent word round to the Gelman that they were retiring. The next; morning we were surprised to hear the column firing at them from our front ; that meant to say that we had got them right in the ee.ntre of us. I can tell you that we gave them beans all that day and at night time we gave them a bit of a chance to run and they did do that, for the next morning our troops walked all over where they had been seen and could not see a live Boer , anywhere,for miles round the place. We had to bury the' dead and bring in the wound=cl ones. I also wish to tell you that it (the relief) was not before its time, that all this came off as we were just beginning to get it a bit rough in Kimberley, as they had brought up some big guns and were playing on tee town with them. They had frightened the people here a bit with them, in fact, we had put the women and children down the mines for safety, but they were something to frighten teem as they had a 100 lb shell dropping in town about two or three hundred times a day, be- sides other small shell which they had just got used to. One of our men had his leg cut straight off , with a splinter from their 100 lb. shell. Tee worst thing was that we had not a gun in the place that would do them any harm. We had a man in Kimnerley who planned the making of a 28'b. gun and as luck would have it when they started with their 1001 b. she'l he was the first man to be killed with a piece of the shell. • Our regiment has been, as yoa' say, very lucky indeed for we have been out fighting six different times and only lost two men killed and 12 wounded but we are not the only ones in Kimberley and the rerneind, r who have been up here have been unlucky for mune Kimberlev we have lost about 60 killed and 70 or ZO wounded. We have lost a lot besides with fever. The worst day of all was the 25h N)vember when we went out and lost 22 killed and a fine sight it was the next day when we were burying them I can tell you. This was also the day when we lost tees of our Regiment. I saw in the paper the contents of my last letter. I think things must be exciting at home, but any way I hope by the time that you get this letter all the excitement will be over. Ido not think that it will last much longer as they seem to be all getting afraid now that we heve ma le one good mon. I have a book here' which I am going to send you, which gives a few details about the siege. I will also send you the piece of a news- paper upen which you will ba able to see all the proclamations and rules under which we have been dur- ing the seiee. In one corner you will see the advertise- ment for the horses which we have been living on and a few more little things so you will see for yourself what it must be like to he beeeiged. I can tell you that it will not be lone before! am at home after this is over that is if I pull through all right which lain living in hopes of doing. I think this is about all this time excepting ask- ing you to give my best respects to all and also receive the same yourself from your son, Sam. Hoping to be , with you in a few more months titne." 1

Inoculation—" Rather Awful."

Mrs. Broadbent of The Elollies,Latchford, has received several letters from her son, Mr. J. W. Broadbent,who is now serving with the 22nd Cempany (Cheshire)lmperial Yeomanry in South Africa. In one of them, which is headed "Maitland, 27th F doruary," he says :—We have arrived at Capetown, and had a busy day yesterday dis- charging all cargo, horses, &o. We marched here in the afternoon, about six miles, verr hot and dusty, with our rifles and all kit. We are 13 in a tent, but get on all right, though there is little room. We have the rug and kit bag for a pillow. This morning at six we started fatigue work, breakfast at eight,ol biscuits,tineed meat, and coffee without sugar or milk. Howevcr we can get a good al in the canteen. Maitland is-a fine place, a plain surrounded by mountains, very dry and sandy. There are about 5 000 troops on the move the whole time ;it is grand. Capetown is a curious place, all white houses, at the base of Table Mountain, end several csmps close to the 502. We got no recepticn ! There were numbers ef ships in port of all sorts, and we could see the Canada with troops on board. lam sitting on my kit bsg at the tent door, smoking a briar, and never felt fitter! it is grand getting asvar from the smell of the steamer. We got splendid graprs at Capetown for 31 a lb. During the voyage we had a good deal of drill, and sometimes practised firing at boxes tlirown overboard ; every fourth day our section did stable work. I often slept on deck,and had a wash under the hose pipe. I washed some clothes on hoard and made a fair job of it. I was inoculated for typhoid. it was rather awful. and I felt very bad neat day, but Dr. Dun kindly took me into his cabin. The firFt thing I had to do this morning was to help to dram away a dead !acme. Most of them look very fit, considering the voyage, but we lost 40 ea the way. It is grand here and a lovely climate. I have eeere,all sorts of troops,from Madras, Caylon,Canada, &o. Horses are always gettine loose and stampeding. There are wire netting paddoeks. all about the camp, full of mules and hersee. Toe Basuto ponies look very nice, but are on;y balf-hrokAn. Our latest orders are for Naauvrport, leaving here in a few days, but there may be changes•X

THE PIANO DISPUTE.--JUDGMENT FOR THE DEFENDANT. —The new trial of the case Crane and Sons, versus Luke Holmes, ordered by Justicls Channell and Bucknill Was heard by his Honour.--Dr. A. P. Liverpool (in- structed by Mr H N Linaker, solicitor, Runcorn) for the defence.—Dr Thomas in opening the case, briefly sub- mitted the facts, with which the police are already familiar, that the piano, for which his clients claimed, was let out by them to Henry John DeKerte, that time licensee of the George Inn, Runcorn, upon agreement of hire purchase. At the time DeKorte left for South Africa, the piano was still upon the premises. Crane and Sons; Alfred Turner, collector testi- fied to seeing the piano &tithe George Inn in August last, and Mr. Charles Tomkine, a reporter, testified to the accuracy of the newspaper report which had been quoted by Counsel--F T the defence, Mr Luke Holmel, the defendant, swore he had only claimed part of the furniture left by DeKorte, and had made no claim to the piano whatever, as be already knew it had been purchased by his sister-in-law, Miss Browning. El never to )k possevion of the house, but simply advised Mrs. Bucannan, to leave the place ; as, on account of the license having lapsed, they might get into trouble with the police. Questiossed by Dr. Thomas as te how he based his claim to the effects of DeKorte. Defen lent did not make any statenoent to him as to owning the goods in the house.—Canisel having addressed the Court, His Honour summed up,and pronounced judgment for the defendant.—The decision carries costs in the appeal case; but the decision upon the c :Ells of the first case remain as the Court ordered.

A RURAL COURTSHIP. A gallant young carter, in corduroys gay, Met the girl that he loved, and to her did say, "Pray, darling, accept me; I give you my heart." She smiled, and said, "John, dear, give me your carte? I'll show it to father; he'll never say 'No,' And then we'll get married, for weal or for woe." John looked quite crestfallen, and said, with a frown, "You know, dearest Julie, the cart's not my own!"

ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football.

[By "Ovalist.l

Warrington once again have a share in the Nor theta Union Cup, and it is within the bounds of possibility that the trophy may find a resting place in the town of soap and wire. The probability of such an event I will not yet discuss, as Warrington's chances are equalled, if not exceeded by some thirty-two other clubs. But the performance of the locals on SaturdaY was decidedly enc,ureging, and they deserve the warmest praise for so smart a win over St. Helens. Somehow I was not much in fear of the "Sainte, though they hold such an excellent record in the Conia petition just concluded. It is entirely the fault of the Warrington men that they have cot done equally aS well ; they have had the capability but they 'have fre- quently lost matches when they could have won had they gone about the Loh in the right way. The know' ledge of this fact inspired many of the Warrington sup' porters with confidence in the team's ability to whack St. Helens if they only tried,—and it was no score!: that they meant to try.

A coup!e of specials, and large numbers if omnibuseS waggonettes, Sze., were required to convey the eager Vtiarringtonians to St Helens. Over a thousand of thent went, and the greatest enthusiasm was shown everr where. There was.enthusiasm in the air—until the snow came at two o'clock and damped it—while everybody at Bank Quay but the station officials, wore the htppfeat and most expansive smiles. The latter gentlemon wore worried looks, and if you asked them innocent questions they looked at you in the fashion that the lion looked at the lamb,prior to the occasion on which U.,‘ first tasted young mutton. It was a dangerous thing to trifle with a booking-clerk, ticket-examiner, or porter on Satur- day, they were all so highly charged with explosive& Nowhere could you find the chastened appearance and lock of serenity born of long suffering. '

The crowd at Knowsley-road was by no means a large one, 3,000 being probibly a fair estimate of the number half of whom came from Warrington. Cup-tie prices of course make a vast difference to those who colisider their pockets b4ore thPir love of the game. As the takings realised £ll6 they could not be called disap- pointing, and the share received by each club would paY, E xpenses and leave a nice balance on the right side. I was glad to see that the St. Helens committee had taken good care of the ground, the rolled surface of which was perfect for playing on. It suited the lighter and speedier Warrington men admirably, and bad a lot to do wi.th the team's succ=29o.

S. Helens were considerably handicapped 1).7 the absence of Foulkes, who occupieo a place on the stand where he nursed a damaged collar-bone. His substitute Mercer was by BO means capable for st:co an important post, and when the Warrington men took to playing on his weakness he frequently let his side down. Warring- ton were without Jones, and Duckworth proved rather weak at centre, but was smart at times. He does not possess the physiqu. for centre play, and was frequently knocked about. Still be was better than Mercer, and the balance of disadvantage rested with S. Helens, plr- ticularly as Traynor wks completely out of form. The effects of hie recent illness seemed very apparent,

The game itself was typical of cup-ties, though for a time Warrington played an open passing game with some success. Very early on it became plain that St. Helens were going to lose unless their play altered a lot, for in every department Warrington were ruining there off their feet. The visiting forwards hee]fd out splen- didly,and the basks put in some speedy combined rune which troubled the home defence greatly. Fish wag very prominent on the left wing and was dangerous every time he got the ball. It was well on towards the interval before there was any score, however, and then it came as a result of speedy following up by Duckworth and Hockenhall. The former captured Liversage, who dropped the ball,and Hockenhall snapped up the oppor- tunity like a trout after a iip. The first forty minuteil ended with Warrington leading by three points.

There is not much to remark about the second moiety Warrington were generally superior, and but for a time when the halves did some shady work at the scrimmage and got penalieed frequently, Warrington attacked per- sistently. It was at this time that Mercer was troubled so greatly by the attentions of biropponents' and from one t.f the favourable positions which Warrington secured near the line, Bate punted across for Fish, who scored after the ball 13,d rebounded from the poet& Though the place was wry emy, Fhb misted the goS for the third or fourth time during the afternoon. St Helens made a spasmodic rush or two but were repelled by Hallam and Isherwood, Warrington entering the second round by the margin of two tries to nit.

The whole of the winning team played splendidly' Hallam in particular silencing his critics by a very sound display. His kicking was exceptionally powerful, and he placed the ball judiciously. Forward, Eden and Mereweather were ever in the van, until the latter sus- tained thd injury which culminated in a fit. The other six were equally good in their OWR style of play, and the fact that they outplayed such a powerful lot as the St. Helens eight is proof of their excellence. Bate and Bur- gess were much superior to the home couple, though the latter's knee troubled him greatly beton the finish. Care will have to be taken of hiln, or he will soon be hors de combat for the rest of this sown.

There waa nothing remarkable in the other results of the first round for the Cup. Widnes and Runcorn both emerged from the fray without a scratch. Oldhara nearly went down at Manningnam, but on replaying the draw on Tuesday they assertul their superiority unmiS- talreably.

Warrington's opponents in the second round are Gcele. who would pkobably have been despised but for the facG that they defeated Flockmr)ndwike last week. As the latter have headed the Yorkshire second cwnpetition for the whole of the season, the perform wen was an excellert one for the 'ktinnors. Warrington should have no difficulty in climbing a step higher, tay.: 'hey should first be cautioned against holding tileir practically utanown foes too cheaply. There will probably be a good gate to-day, for Warrington's success at St. Helens has vastly pleased the club's supporters.

At Birmingham Ass'zeq. Wednesday, a youth named Bell was awarded £750 (iamages against the Cor- plraticn of Smetlawiek and Supetintendent Forater, of the fire brigade. In celebration of the incorporation of the borough on November 9th a cannon was fired in the public wk. and Bell was ii,jnred and lost an eye. A well-informed correspond,nt wires that the Govern. ment proposes to re-arm the, po-;itiou batteries of the Volunteer Artillery with 47 guns on specially designed mountings for mobility.

A County Court Bailiff's Patriotism. Before hearing the disputed cases at the Warrington County Court last Thursday,his LiGnour Judge Gwy nut- James said he understood that Mr. Newton, who was one of the bailiffs of the Court had under the lost Army order been called up. L'; was optional to him uuder that order whether he responded to the call or not, but if he did so he was liab'e to serve in Ertiz- land, for twelve months. Mr Newton wished to know whet his position in that court would be when he re- turned in twelve moaths' time. MeNewton might make his mind perfectly easy in reference to that matter. If he served for 12 months, at the end of !hat period he (his Honour) should at owe reappoint him in his position. Everyone must admire the way in hich t mployers of labour bad kept places epen for servants who had had to go out to do their outy for their country and he sh,,uld be the last person to refuse to offer the same privilege to Mr Newton when he cathe back. (Hear, hear.) It is interesting to note tnat Mr. Newton is a sr:!olier who has seen considerable active service. In 1879-80 he s<rved in the Manchester Regiment with Lord Roberts in Afghanistan and took part in Ihe eel t brated march tr.m Cabal to Kandahar In 1882 he was in E:rypt with Lord Wolsley's forces and took part in the battle of Tel-el-Kebir. He was present at the surrender of Arabi Pasha. He has two medals and holds a certificate fc,r being proficient in the Hincu4tani language. He held the appointment in India of instructor in musktry , of the 16th Bengal native infantry and alto the position of Government Librarian. Be has now responded to the Qu; en's appeAi to her old soldiers to ofLr thomelves for hums dt-fence services. A CHEAP CIGAR. There are plenty of cheap cigars but in ninety-nine cases out of a hundred the cigar sold at a low price is not worth being called a cigar. It is a cigar by name but not by nature, and the purchaser usually has grave doubts about its cheapness by the time he has got down to the last half-inch. We have no doubt that a good cigar can be put on the market at a reasonable figure, and tire proprieors of the "London Weekly Times and Echo" have proved this by making it an accomplished fat. They are now issuing a " Weekly Times and Eoho " cigar at 2d. or 50 for 83. 44., specially manufac-Ared by Messri. Wart- mann and Co., and the nurchacer of the article will, we feel sure, consider he has got hi a money's worth. Hav- ing tried them we can cordially recommend the "Weekly Times and Echo " Cigars. They are mild, cool, sweet, and the public and the trade will find them a good in- vestment. 110 - Warrington Markets. OUGIITRINCIT-)N PARE —At War rington, on Saturday, before a moderate attendance of spectators. Onahtrington were the first to pressabut the Barnton defence was sound. Gradually Bunton worked their way inta Ouantrington's territory, but the point scored by them was disallowed. After exchanges Ough- trington broke away, and Barnton being penalised Gib- bons headed a pretty goat. Barnton attacked without success, and was confined to midfield. Barnton main- tained the attack, and Oughtriugtou's defence Was severely tried. The game was of give-and-take nature. If anything Barnton tiad the best,bat strict attention to their defence caused a little uneasiness to the Ough- trills-stop defence. A corner kick availen Barnton little, and from an accurate centre the Park right wing cleared cleverly. Oughtrirseton broke away but Barnton prover too good. A corner fer Banton ended in a rush for their goal, but nathing resulted. Half-time : Ough trington, one; Barnton United nil. The second half saw Barnton early equalize as a result of clever forward play Becket': scoring a fine goal. Even play followed, chiefly in favour of the United, but nothing further occurred, and the final score was Bunton United one goal ; Ough- trington Park, one goal. WARRINGTON FIELD CLUB. -- Annual Meeting. The annual and entomological meeting was held in the Museum Lecture-room, when the rows of tables pro- vided for the purpose were speedily crowded with Poxes • containing specimens of last summer's captures. Those who remember the inception of the Field Club sixteen years ago and the good work done by its founders and che first generation of members can testify that there has been no falling off in energy or . enthusiasm Frc,rn the outset severe criticism 02 c-reless or sloveuly work has been a note of this thosouelsly democratic , society, whilst the more experienced members have lal ways extended ready help to beginners, and endeavoured to combine with theinstinet of the collector habits of observe:ems aid an intelligent love of the veriaus species of the groap at whica the young student may be work eg. One direction in which friendly • emulation has borne remarkable fruit is the mounting an.i setting of insects. Only those who have tried can reanze the patience and skill required to properly display moths and butterflies, whose delicate scales can scarcely be touched without injury, or beetles so small that their legs and antennse can only be seen with a lens ; yet in all the rows of boxes, among et many hundreds of insects, whether exhialted be veterans or recraits, it would have bent difficult to find a badly set specnnen• Messrs. F. WOMERSLEY and W. MOUNFIELD read notes on the season's work, and elieiteet anscussioa on the erratic occurrence of colitis edusa. Mr L Greening re- marked that about 25 years ago it occurred in profusion both at Sankey and Orford, he having taken 20 or 30 at a time of both •exes; then for many years this morn was not again seen in the locality. Mr J Collins showed some beeutinal clear rings. A rare black variety of the com- mon gooseberry moth from Mr Tunstall's collection was much admirer!, as also Was a specimen case, one of a s ri s, exhibited by the Museum Committee, designed to convey clear an_l aecurate intormatioa by a combine-. tiou of carefully-worded labels and illustrative typical forms. Mr ',ewe exhibited a number of diptera, and Dr , Bunting boxes of hemiptera and coleeptera.Of the donacia, 1 water beetles with a curious trick of clinging to the leaves of aquatic plants, there are 19 British species. Tne indefatigable doctor has taken 18 of these in Cheshire, and in adlitioa to his previaus soccesses discovered another staphylinid beetle, new to Britain, at Wallasey last summer. An intimation that Dr. Billups would ere long remove to another part of the country evoked strong expressions of regret that one who hal accpired such an extensive knowledge of the flora and fauna of the dis- trict, and done so ranch to promote its intelligent study, 1 should leave Warrington. He was unanimously reques- ted to prepare a list of the district flora,a task for which no one else is so competent, and, whilst deprecating the eulogistic terms iu which the request was made, he promised to undertake this, aad antioanced, amidst loud applause, that sooner or later his collections would Skid a resting place in the Warrington Museum. Votes of thanks were accorded to the Museum Com- mittee to the retiring Chairman (Councillor J Shaw Green) and to the Hon. Secretary, (Councillor 9 J Jolley) Dr. White was elected chairman for the ensuing year, and Mr. Jolley was re-elected secretary. A brief con- versation noon the arrangements for the summer pro- gramme concluded a meeting which was alive from start to finish. The "good old town "is evidently as much to the fore in scientific pursuits as it is in com- merce, in local or in the larger Imperial patriotism.

WARRINGTON WESLEYAN CIRCUIT. , Quarterly Meeting. In connection with the Warriegton Wesleyan Circuit the quarterly meeting was held at Bald-street Wesleyan Church on Monday evening. At five o'clock a good number sac down to tea, at the conclusion of wnich hearty votes of thanks wen., accorded to Mrs. J. Taylor and Mrs. J. W. Hey ward,for their kindness in arranging for the tea. The Rev. Theodore Bishop,superintendent, presided over the meeting and amongst others present were the ministers uf the circuit, the Revs C El Hocken, El G Roberts, M.A., and J Gunnell,the circuit stewards, Messrs Jelin Taylor, and J W Hayward, J.P. ; Miss Bessie Dutton and Messrs R G Elenshail, Wigfield, W Brough, Horton, Weaver, Wright,Winstanley, Councillor Bennett, J Bennett, Hickman, Gandy, Bailey, Dale, Pendlehurv, Metdrum, Roylance,Smith, Barlow,Perkins, Basnetf, and W Silcock, secretary. The first business of the meeting was a resolution to' send a message of sympathy to Mrs Lawton on the death of her husband, the late Mr W Y Lawton. The financial statement was presented by Mr J Taylor and was adopted as satisfac- tory. The returns of numters of those meeting in class showed a decrease on the year among adults, wite a good number on trial for membership and an increase in the junior classes. The Revs. T Bishop, C H Hocken, H G Roberts, and J Gunnell were cordially thanked for their past services, and unanimously invited to remain in the circuit. The stewards were appointed ex officio to attend the Liver- pool District Synod at Southport, next May,and Messrs W Silcock and W Brough were elected to attend as representatives, and if these should be unable to attend Messrs Wigfield and Davey. The superintendent was authorised to invite the Synod te meet In Warrington, in May, 1901. The temperance, education, and worn out ministers' fund schedules were not complete, and are to be presented at the June meeting. The foreign mission collections for the year amounted to £2OO 18s 7d, a decrease on 1898-9 of £lO 184 10d. Bew.ey-road had a substantial increase, but there was a falling off in Padgate, Lymm, and in some ether village churches. There was a discussion on the introduction of the weekly offertory at Penketh and Padgate, and on chapel affairs at Stockton Heath, Martinscroft, and Hatton. Mr. Roylanee (Moore) gave an account of the position at Hatton. Messrs R G Hen- shall, W Silcock, Meldrum, J Shaw-Green and C Parker were appointed a committee to examine and report on the financial affairs of the circuit to the June meeting. The meeting expressed its deep sympathy with the sufferers by the famine in India, and resolved that col- lections should be made on behalf of the fund for their relief. It was decided to bold a meeting in June at Lyrnm.
